Orders)，VII，第1—421页。——利 (Lea)：《宗教裁判所史》(Hist. of the Inquisition)，I，第242—304页。——M. 克赖顿 (M. Creighton)：《托钵修士的到来》(The Coming of the Friars)，载《讲演集》(Lectures and Addresses)，第69—84页。——A. 杰索普 (A. Jessopp)：《托钵修士的到来》(The Coming of the Friars)。——史蒂文森 (Stevenson)：《格罗斯泰斯特传》(Life of Grosseteste)，伦敦，1899年，第59—87页。——豪克 (Hauck)，IV，第366—483页。

关于圣方济各近年文献的说明。 萨巴蒂耶 (Sabatier) 的传记于1804年出版，给圣方济各生平的研究带来了异乎寻常的推动。卡尔·米勒 (Karl Müller) 将这部传记置于“现代史学著述的顶峰”，见《文学报》(Lit.-zeitung)，1895年，第179—186页。此书对文献的驾驭达到了前所未有的程度，并对这位意大利圣徒的精神怀有深切的共鸣。它彻底改变了新教徒对他的看法，向世人展示了真实方济各的准确形象。令人惊异的是，一位新教牧师竟成了现代研究方济各的首要学者，也是他最忠诚的敬仰者之一！萨巴蒂耶继其首部著作之后，又不倦地考察圣方济各与方济各会的早期文献和历史；为此，他辞去了牧职，一次又一次前往意大利，并在亚西西 (Assisi) 长期逗留。在那里，他备受尊敬，人们向游客指点他，仿佛他也是当地主要名胜之一。他在文献发现上颇为幸运；作为编者，他开创了一套新的方济各会文献。他的热忱与劳作激励了德国、意大利和瑞士的一批学者专门研究早期方济各会文献，如米诺基 (Minocchi)、马多内 (Madonnet)、米勒、莱姆普 (Lempp) 和施努雷尔 (Schnürer)。他的《圣方济各传》被列入《禁书目录》(Index)，理由据说是它曲解了天主教的习俗。

萨巴蒂耶对方济各生平和性格的描写，除在方济各会士中以外，可以说已获得普遍认可；然而，对于早期文献的年代及其原始内容，人们仍有很大分歧。彼此差异甚大的手稿相继刊布，加上学者们各异的批判意见，使这一课题的文献问题变得极为复杂。米勒在《文学报》(Lit.-zeitung)，1902年，第593页，以及莱姆普在《文学报》(Lit. zeitung)，1906年，第509页，都把这种混乱比作几乎看不出路径的丛莽。这种混乱源于格列高利九世 (Gregory IX) 和早期方济各会住院派 (conventual wing) 坚决推行的一项政策：销毁一切表明方济各反对会内严密纪律、坚持绝对贫穷会规的材料。1264年的方济各会总议会下令，销毁此前写成的所有方济各传记，唯独保留波纳文图拉 (Bonaventura) 所著的一部。方济各对绝对贫穷会规的坚持、最初的会规，以及他的遗嘱，都要被彻底抹去。萨巴蒂耶以其明敏的眼光所开创的新研究，追溯到1264年以前，重新找回了真实方济各的形象。

学者的注意力主要集中于萨巴蒂耶1898年刊布的《全德之镜》(Speculum perfectionis)，以及方济各第三会的原始会规。《全德之镜》是一部方济各传记；据萨巴蒂耶所说（导论，第li页），它是最早的一部传记，可追溯至1227年。这份文献的发现，是近代历史发现中最有趣、最引人注目的事件之一。它被发现的经过如下：——

方济各的传记材料见于一部题为《圣方济各及其同伴生平之镜》(Speculum vitae St. Francisci et sociorum ejus) 的书，1504年初版于威尼斯，1509年再版于巴黎。萨巴蒂耶研究1509年巴黎版时，发现其中有118章未署作者姓名，其精神与文体均异于其余部分。他在撰写传记时采用了这份文献，并倾向于将它归于方济各的三位同伴——利奥 (Leo)、安杰洛 (Angelo) 和鲁菲诺 (Rufino)。见《圣方济各传》(Vie de S. François)，第lxxii页及下页。后来，他发现，在几部手稿中，这些章节被标为一份独立文献。在马扎然图书馆 (Mazarin library) 的手稿中，他发现了124个独立章节，其中包括1509年巴黎版的那16章。萨巴蒂耶认为，这些章节构成一部独立著作，即利奥所写的《全德之镜》，是有关方济各生平和教导的最早著述。其成书年代取自马扎然手稿，所载日期为MCCXXVIII。萨巴蒂耶认为，文献本身的一些迹象也证实了这一日期，见第xxii页等。

这部满怀同情、明晰而坦率的叙述，以新的面貌呈现了方济各：他是格列高利九世野心谋划的牺牲者。格列高利废弃了方济各心中最为珍重的绝对贫穷会规，又派一个代表他本人教皇观念的人居于方济各之上。萨巴蒂耶主张（导论，第li页），利奥是在科尔托纳的埃利亚 (Elias of Cortona) 宣布打算在这位“小穷人”的墓上兴建一座宏伟主教座堂之后，立即写成此书的。利奥无法抑制自己的愤慨，于是发出抗议，反对如此粗暴地摆布方济各的计划及其身后形象。

萨巴蒂耶对《全德之镜》(Speculum perfectionis) 年代的判定，遭到了严重质疑。蒂勒曼 (Tilemann)、格茨 (Goetz) 等人与米诺基一致，采用奥尼桑蒂 (Ognissanti，佛罗伦萨的一座修道院) 手稿所载的日期，即MCCCXVII，并通过仔细研究其他方济各传记，得出结论，认为《全德之镜》是一部汇编。不过，他们同意，其中某些内容早于切拉诺的托马斯 (Thomas a Celano) 的《第二传》(Vita secunda)，即方济各的第二部传记，或年代更为久远。米勒在《文学报》(Lit.-zeitung)，1899年，第49—52页，1902年，第598页，以及莱姆普，虽不接受1227年这一早期年代，却将这份文献定于十三世纪上半叶，并视之为第一等的权威史料，eine Quelle ersten Ranges。莱姆普说，它深入洞察了方济各真实的心思和灵魂，见《文学报》(Lit.-zeitung)，1905年，第9页及下页。蒂勒曼也认为这份文献具有极高价值。米诺基、范奥特罗伊 (van Ortroy) 等人在各评论刊物发表的众多文章，见蒂勒曼：《全德之镜》(Speculum perfectionis)，第4页。

如果萨巴蒂耶向我们展示的确是历史上真实的方济各——我们有理由相信确是如此——那么，方济各在乌戈利诺枢机 (Cardinal Ugolino)，即格列高利九世的巧妙操纵下丧失权柄的一幕，便是历史上最令人心酸的场景之一；而方济各也就显明为基督教历代最无私、心灵最纯洁的人物之一。

§ 70. 亚西西的圣方济各

“自他光荣诞生，未过多久，

蒙赐非凡德行的

这轮奇异太阳，便开始慰藉大地，

他尚在幼年，便承受父亲的怒气，

只为那人人如死亡般厌恶、

从心愿之门外逐走的女子，

在天庭之前，也在

他父亲面前，他娶她为妻；

从此一日比一日更加爱她，

她失去第一位夫君以后，

已有一千二百年被遗弃、无人知晓，

无人求婚，直到他来到。

但恐我的话不够明白，

须知我说的这一对爱侣，

从此指的就是方济各与贫穷。”

——但丁 (Dante)：《天堂篇》(Paradiso) XI，赖特 (Wright) 译本。

在圣徒传记的名录上，方济各会创始人亚西西的方济各 (Francis of Assisi) 名列前茅。在所有意大利圣徒中，无论在意大利境内还是境外，他都是最受欢迎的一位。256

弗朗切斯科——即方济各——贝尔纳尔多内 (Francesco—Francis—Bernardone，1182—1226年)，生于亚西西，也卒于此地。他的洗名是乔万尼 (Giovanni)，即约翰；方济各这个名字，似乎是他父亲彼得罗·贝尔纳尔多内 (Pietro Bernardone) 为他取的，与法国有关，因为这位富有的布商常到法国经商。方济各学过拉丁文，对书写之术却只粗通。他有钱可花，便将钱用在寻欢作乐上。在亚西西与佩鲁贾 (Perugia) 的一次战争中，他从军入伍，遭到俘虏。获释时，他二十二岁。此后的一场疾病，开始唤醒他的宗教天性。他病愈起身时，已厌弃自己，对世界也不再满足。他再次入伍，启程前往意大利南部投奔布里安的瓦尔特 (Walter of Brienne)，走到了斯波莱托 (Spoleto)。但他命定要走的并非军人的道路。在严肃信念的感召下，他折返故乡，退居亚西西附近的一个洞穴，寻求独处。他曾往罗马朝圣，是否为了补赎，则不得而知。他开始同情穷人。他遇见一个麻风病人，起初惊恐退缩，却又转身回来，亲吻那人的手，并将身上所有的钱都放在他手里。他常到故乡城郊的小堂去，流连最多的却是圣达米安堂 (St. Damian)；那是一座简陋的小堂，陈设粗陋，只有一位神父供职。这里成了他灵魂的伯特利 (Bethel)。在那粗陋的祭坛前，他似乎听见了基督的声音。他热心大发，从父亲那里取了货物，送给神父。据我们所知，方济各从未觉得自己应当为这一行为悔改。这里所见的道德标准，与我们的不同。例如，塞缪尔·约翰逊博士 (Dr. Samuel Johnson) 的心境是何等不同！他为了曾经违逆父亲的一件事，在成年之后，竟光着头，站在利奇菲尔德 (Litchfield) 露天广场的雨中忏悔！

这位昔日纵情享乐的青年所经历的转变，使方济各遭到全城的嘲笑和父亲无情的愤怒。他被逐出父家。他没有流露出我们认为一个儿子在类似处境下理当有的任何惋惜之情，反而公开声明解除自己为人子的义务，说：“直到如今，我一直称彼得罗·贝尔纳尔多内为父亲；但现在我只愿事奉上帝，除‘我们在天上的父’以外，不再称任何人为父。”从此，方济各便献身于宗教生活。他衣着单薄，住在麻风病人中间，为他们洗涤疮口，并在城中的广场和街道上乞求石料，修复圣达米安堂。这是在1208年。

此时，苏巴西奥山 (Mt. Subasio) 的本笃会院长将天使圣母小堂 (Santa Maria degli Angeli) 赠给了方济各。257 这座小堂以“宝尊堂” (Portiuncula，意为“小小的一份”) 之名，成为这位圣徒及其早期同伴最钟爱的圣所。方济各的大多数异象是在这里见到的，他也在这里去世。258 后来，他从洪诺留三世 (Honorius III) 那里取得一项非同寻常的特许：每年自8月1日晚祷至8月2日晚祷之间，凡来此小堂朝拜的人，都可获全大赦。这使宝尊堂成为首屈一指的朝圣地。

1209年，方济各听见了这些话：“传道，说天国近了；医治病人，洁净麻风病人，赶出鬼。你们的钱袋里不要带金、银、铜。”他便丢下手杖、钱袋和鞋子，以这些使徒的训令为自己生活的准则。他宣讲悔改，身边聚集了贝尔纳尔多·迪·昆塔瓦洛 (Bernardo di Quintavallo)、埃吉迪奥 (Egidio) 以及其他同伴。命人守贫穷、背十字架的三段经文——《马太福音》十六章24—26节、十九章21节和《路加福音》九章1—6节——成了他们的会规。259 这会规所要求的，无非是完全顺服福音。他们后来被称为“小兄弟”——fratres minores；凡无法靠劳作糊口的地方，他们便挨家乞讨，赤足行走，260 睡在草料阁楼、麻风病院，以及任何可以栖身的地方。

他们应当传道，但尤其应当以生活体现福音的训诫。生活是最重要的事，比讲道和学问更重要。方济各担心学问会毁掉谦卑。有一个妇人前来求施舍，他便将他们晨祷时诵读的一部《新约》送给她；那是当时修院里唯一的一本书。修院甚至连一本日课经都没有。261 方济各所要强调的，是充满善行与同情的生活。在遗嘱中，方济各称自己是一个不学之人，idiota。切拉诺的托马斯 (Thomas à Celano) 也如此称呼他。这个词似乎兼有两层意思：未经教育的人，以及仅受过初等教育的人。它也用来指平信徒，以区别于教士。方济各的教育仅限于基础学业，而他的传记作者一再强调，他是直接受上帝教导的。262 方济各的亲笔文字现存两件，一件在亚西西，一件在斯波莱托。263

1210年，方济各与几个同伴前往罗马，受到英诺森三世 (Innocent III) 的接见。264 据那位英国编年史家记载，教皇为试验他的诚意，说：“去吧，弟兄，到猪群那里去；与你相比，猪比人更相称。去和它们一起打滚，向它们宣讲你如此善于阐述的规条吧。”方济各遵命而行，回来后说：“我主，我已经照办了。”265 教皇于是为这个兄弟会祝福，非正式地认可他们的会规，准许他们行削发礼，并吩咐他们出去宣讲悔改。

兄弟会迅速壮大。会士都应当劳动。方济各在遗嘱中劝勉弟兄们，像他自己一样从事某种手艺。他把闲懒的修士比作雄蜂。266 弟兄们探望病人，尤其是麻风病人，传道的范围不断扩大，也到国外从事宣教之旅。方济各宁愿卖掉祭坛上的饰物，也不愿拒绝求助的人。每逢看见比自己更穷的人，他便感到羞愧。267 就在这一时期，方济各生涯中最非凡的事件之一发生了：他与贫穷结了婚。他称贫穷为自己的新娘、母亲、姊妹，并始终以骑士般的忠诚爱慕她。268 故事是这样说的：方济各与几个同伴出去寻找贫穷。两位老人指点他们，说她住在一座高山上。在那里，贫穷坐在“她匮乏的宝座上”接待他们；方济各赞颂她是主形影不离的伴侣，是“众美德的主母与女王”。贫穷回答说，她曾与亚当同在乐园里，但在人堕落之后，她便成了无家可归的流浪者，直到主来，将她赐给他的选民。借着她，信徒的人数大大增加；但过了一些时候，她的姊妹逼迫夫人离开了她。信徒失去了坚忍。后来，修士们来与她相伴，但她的仇敌贪婪却冒用审慎之名，使修士富足起来。最终，修道生活完全屈服于世俗，贫穷也就彻底离开了它。如今，方济各与贫穷结合；她以平安之吻亲吻他和他的同伴，与他们一同下山。一个新的时代开始了。从此，这些朋友以石为枕，以饼和水为食，以世界为修院。269

1212年，希菲家的克拉拉 (Clara of Sciffi) 进入了方济各的生活。她比他小十二岁，十六岁时第一次在亚西西主教座堂听他讲道。那篇讲道深入了她的灵魂。在方济各的帮助下，她逃离父家，并由他接纳发愿入修会。270 他将她带到一所本笃会女修院。她的妹妹阿格内斯 (Agnes) 随后也追随克拉拉而来。圣达米安小堂被划归她们使用，克拉拉会 (Clarisses) 就在那里创立。克拉拉比方济各活得更久，于1253年在利奥 (Leo)、安杰洛 (Angelo) 和吉内弗罗 (Ginefro) 几位弟兄在场时去世。

1217年，方济各被引见到洪诺留三世及教廷面前。听从枢机主教乌戈利诺 (Ugolino，即后来的格列高利九世 [Gregory IX]) 的劝告，他预先准备并背熟了讲章。但来到教皇面前，他却忘记了准备好的内容，于是即席讲论，赢得了与会众人的心。

方济各在意大利各地巡回布道，1219年更远赴埃及和叙利亚。从东方回来时，这位小穷人——il poverello——发现，在严厉的纪律维护者乌戈利诺的影响下，兄弟会中已引进了一种新的因素。这场剧变使他余下的岁月笼罩在深重的忧伤中，尽管他几乎不曾表露出来。1220年途经博洛尼亚 (Bologna) 时，他看见有人正在为弟兄们建造房屋，内心极其痛苦。枢机主教乌戈利诺已决意操纵这个团体，使它为教廷的利益服务。他曾向方济各提出援助，方济各也接受了。在这位枢机主教的影响下，1221年通过了新的会规，1223年又通过了第三部，其中方济各独特的愿望被置之不顾。最初关于贫穷的会规被修改，旧有的修道纪律观念被引入，又增添了绝对服从教皇的新条款。方济各的心思过于单纯质朴，无法与精明的教会统治者周旋。从此，教会权势人物的策略支配了修会。271 方济各被排挤到一旁，一位法学博士、贵族出身的彼得罗·迪·卡塔纳 (Pietro di Catana) 被任为总会长，置于团体之首。这就是方济各从叙利亚回来时所见的局面。他接受了这一切，对弟兄们说：“从今以后，对你们来说，我已经死了。这位彼得·迪·卡塔纳弟兄，你们和我都要服从他。”随后，他俯伏在地，向取代自己的人许诺顺服与归从。272

方济各这样被迫自居人下，构成了中世纪传记中最动人的场景之一。方济各原本不愿求取教皇的特权。他所赞同的是行动的自由。乌戈利诺却以娴熟的手腕，代之以严格的修道服从。组织将取代自发的虔敬。无疑，乌戈利诺不仅口称是方济各的朋友，也确实是他的朋友。他为纪念方济各而在亚西西奠立大教堂的根基，并在他死后两年将他封为圣徒。但他并不理解方济各的精神。从此，方济各已无力实行自己最初的理想；273 然而，他虽在外表上毫无违命的表现，却始终坚定地持守这些理想，直到生命的终结。

这些理想在方济各著名的遗嘱中得到重申。这份文件是基督教文献中最感人至深的篇章之一。在这里，方济各自称“小弟兄”，frater parvulus。他能留给弟兄们的，只有自己的祝福、兄弟会早期岁月的回忆，以及持守最初会规的劝告。这会规并非得自任何人间的教师；是全能的上帝亲自向他启示，他应当按照神圣福音的方式生活。他提醒他们，最初的会士何等喜爱住在贫寒而荒废的教堂里。他嘱咐他们，不可接受教堂或房屋，除非这与他们所誓守的神圣贫穷之规相合。他禁止他们从教廷领受谕令，即使是为保护自身也不例外。同时，他又保证顺服总会长，表明自己决意不违背他的意愿去任何地方、做任何事，“因为他是我的主人”。整份文件中始终回荡着一缕悲痛之音。274

方济各的心碎了。他的身体向来不强健，晚年更是疾病缠身。更换居处只能带来暂时的缓解。医生采用了当时所知的治疗方法，将一块烧得白热的烙铁贴在方济各的额头上。方济各起初畏缩，但仍接受了治疗，说：“火弟兄，你比一切受造物都美丽，此刻请善待我。”他诙谐地称自己的身体为“驴弟兄”。275 民众对他的崇敬超越了一切限度。他们争抢他的衣服碎片、头发，甚至剪下的指甲。

去世前两年，方济各写成了《太阳颂》 (Canticle to the Sun)，勒南 (Renan) 称之为现代宗教情感最完美的表达。276 写作之时，他正受到种种试探的困扰，眼睛也开始失明。这首赞歌，是对自然炽烈之爱的虔诚迸发。它超越了中世纪其他一切田园诗。事实上，方济各对自然的热爱，在他那个时代的记载中实属罕见；这使他与现代那一大群在云中看见诗篇、在花间听见交响乐的人为伍。他爱树木、石头、飞鸟和田野的植物。在万物之中，他尤其爱太阳和火：太阳受造，为要在白昼照亮我们的眼睛；火则在夜间赐我们光明，因为“上帝借着这两位弟兄，照亮了我们的眼睛”。

方济各也有信息要传给无理性的受造物，并向飞鸟讲道。有一次他说：“鸟弟兄们，你们应当十分爱你们的创造主，赞美他。他赐给你们羽毛作衣裳，赐给你们翅膀飞翔，又赐给你们一切有用之物。你们不必种，也不必收，他却看顾你们。”鸟儿便曲着颈项望着他，仿佛在向他致谢。他还希望皇帝制定一项特别法律，禁止杀害或伤害“我们的姊妹——飞鸟”。277 后来的传说讲述了许多关于他支配自然之能力的奇事，278 例如驯服古比奥 (Gubbio) 的恶狼。这狼使附近居民闻之丧胆。它张着口向方济各扑来，方济各却说：“狼弟兄，奉耶稣基督的名，我命令你不可伤害我，也不可伤害任何人。”它便如羔羊一般伏在他脚前。方济各应许赦免它过去的一切恶行，条件是它不再伤人。这野兽低下头，在他面前屈膝，表示接受这约定。此后，它成了古比奥人宠爱的动物。

在生命的最后一周，这位圣徒让人一再向他诵读以“我发声哀告耶和华”开头的《诗篇》第142篇，以及他自己的《太阳颂》。他召来安杰洛 (Angelo) 和利奥 (Leo) 两位弟兄，为他歌唱死亡姊妹。279 科尔托纳的埃利亚斯 (Elias of Cortona) 曾协助罗马教廷废置方济各最初的会规，此时出言劝阻，说民众会认为临终时如此欢快，有失圣徒的身份。但方济各回答说，他两年来一直思想死亡，如今他与主如此紧密地联合，理当在主里面喜乐。280 因此，正如切拉诺的托马斯 (Thomas à Celano) 所说，“他歌唱着迎接死亡”。281 弟兄们应他的请求，将他抬到波尔齐翁科拉 (Portiuncula) 小堂。途中，他要求将床转过来，好让自己的脸再一次朝向亚西西。他已不能看见，却仍能祷告，于是便为这座城向上天祈求。282 到了教堂，他与弟兄们一同擘饼，亲口行了祭司的职事。1226年10月3日，用利奥弟兄的话说，他“迁往主耶稣基督那里；他曾全心爱主，并且最完全地跟随主”。

棺盖尚未合上，极大的尊荣便已开始加在这位圣洁之人身上。亚西西市民接管了他的遗体，而方济各的名字也成为这座风景如画、静谧欲睡的古城最吸引人的所在。两年后，他被封为圣徒。283 典礼于1228年7月26日在亚西西举行，格列高利九世 (Gregory IX.) 亲自出席。次日，教皇为纪念方济各而兴建的新大教堂奠基。1243年，英诺森四世 (Innocent IV.) 为教堂举行奉献礼，方济各的遗体安放在主祭坛下。284 契马布埃 (Cimabue) 和乔托 (Giotto) 的艺术作品装饰着圣堂内部。堂前现代雕塑家迪普雷 (Dupré) 的雕像，表现这位伟大的托钵修士身穿本会会服，双臂交叠胸前，俯首而立的形象。方济各刚刚去世，科尔托纳的埃利亚斯便作出关于圣痕 (stigmata) 的惊人宣告。据说，方济各身上带着这些痕迹，与基督被钉十字架时身上的五处伤口相应。方济各身上的圣痕是肉质的突起，却不带血。事情的叙述如下：在一段禁食并全神贯注于祈祷的时期，圣十字架节的清晨，基督在初升的太阳中向方济各显现，形如一位展开翅膀、被钉在十字架上的撒拉弗。异象消失之后，方济各感到双手和肋旁疼痛。他领受了圣痕 (stigmata)。这事发生于1224年，地点是韦尔纳山 (Verna)，285 位于阿尔诺河 (Arno) 上游，海拔三千英尺。

证明这些痕迹确实存在的历史证据如下。方济各去世的次日，科尔托纳的埃利亚斯以修会代理总会长的身份，向各地方济各会士致函，宣布他曾在方济各身上看见圣痕 (stigmata)。信中有这样的话：“除了在上帝之子身上，世人从未见过这样的记号。在去世以前很长一段时间，我们的弟兄身上便有五处伤口，确实是基督的圣痕 (stigmata)；因为他的双手双脚，内外都有仿佛钉痕的印记，是一种疤痕，而他的肋旁仿佛被枪刺透，有少许血渗出。”《完美之镜》 (Speculum Perfectionis) 或许是方济各最早的传记，在叙述他所遭受的严酷试探时，虽是顺带提及这些圣痕，却说得十分明确。286 切拉诺的托马斯至迟在1230年便已作了较详尽的描述，并声称方济各尚在人世时，就有少数人见过这些圣痕。1237年，格列高利九世号召全教会承认这些圣痕，并谴责多明我会士质疑其真实性。287 方济各最早的画像绘于1236年，也画出了这些痕迹。

另一方面，反对其真实性的一项极有力的论据，是格列高利九世在1228年册封方济各为圣徒的诏书中，对这些圣痕只字未提。我们会认为，方济各配受圣徒之名，莫过于以此为最好的凭据，而这一遗漏实在无法解释。288

假定方济各身上确实有这些伤痕，人们对圣痕提出了三种解释。1. 它们出于超自然的神迹。这是罗马天主教的看法。1304年，本笃十一世 (Benedict XI.) 设立了圣痕节。2. 它们是默想十字架上的基督所引起的高度激动的精神状态的产物。这是萨巴蒂埃 (Sabatier) 的看法。289 3. 第三种解释认为，这是方济各自己施行的虔诚欺骗；他渴望感受基督所受的一切痛苦，便用手指在自己身上抠出这些痕迹。290 这样的做法似乎令人难以置信。既然没有足够的道德理由说明为何要印上这些圣痕，持批判态度的人就难以接受。另一方面，历史见证又如此有力，令人若要否认，也须费一番力气。就我们所知，方济各从未以圣痕来证明自己的使命。291

研究同时代人所叙述的亚西西的方济各 (Francis d’Assisi) 的生平，并从他自己的临终遗嘱中体察其精神，所留下的印象，是志向纯洁、心灵谦卑——是真正的圣洁。他不求尊位，也不求跻身显贵之列。他怀着单纯的心，力求重新宣扬福音的训诫，并以自身的榜样将其付诸实践，借此服事同胞。他力求将福音重新传给平民百姓，而百姓也乐意听他。他或许没有强大的才智。他缺乏教会外交家的禀赋，却无疑有一颗炽热的心；他对世人燃烧不息的爱，使他的人格具有感召力。他不是神学思想家，却是一个对人怀有切实宗教关怀、且言行相符的人。他的言语和行动，显出他对上帝所托付的使命满怀信心。292 他向教会发出呼声；此后直到路德 (Luther) 出现，再没有人像他这样说话。

历史上很少有人像方济各那样，给人留下如此深刻的印象。他的人格在当时光照远近，而他的使命却延及历代。他并未因抗议礼仪或教义而成为自己时代的异乡人，却因使徒般的淳朴和天真温柔，在各个时代都令人感到亲切。我们对他的钦佩，并不化为对一个理想人生完美典范的崇奉。方济各的虔敬，毕竟带着中世纪的色彩。然而，就我们所能知道的，他在历代领悟基督话语的意义、流露基督精神的人中，堪称佼佼者。因此，哈纳克 (Harnack) 能称他为“亚西西那位奇妙的圣徒”，萨巴蒂埃也给予他崇高的赞誉，说“他蒙恩得以洞见属灵祭司职分的优越”。293

至高、全能、良善的主上帝啊，赞美、荣耀、尊贵和一切颂赞都归于你！

愿我的主上帝因他的一切受造物而受赞美，尤其因我们的太阳弟兄；他带给我们白昼，带给我们光明；他美丽，发出极大的光辉：主啊，他向我们表明你！

愿我的主因我们的月亮姊妹，并因众星而受赞美；他使它们在天上明亮而美丽。

愿我的主因我们的风弟兄，因空气、云彩、晴朗及各种天气而受赞美；你借着它们维系万物的生命。

愿我的主因我们的水姊妹而受赞美；她对我们大有用处，谦卑、宝贵而洁净。

愿我的主因我们的火弟兄而受赞美；你借着他在黑暗中赐给我们光明；他明亮、可爱，强劲而有力。

愿我的主因我们的大地母亲而受赞美；她养育我们，护持我们，生出各样果实、五彩缤纷的花朵和青草。

愿我的主因一切为他的爱而彼此饶恕、忍受软弱与患难的人而受赞美；那些平静忍耐的人有福了，因为至高者啊，你必赐他们冠冕。

愿我的主因我们的姊妹——肉身的死亡——而受赞美，无人能逃脱她。在死罪中死去的人有祸了！被发现遵行至圣旨意的人有福了，因为第二次的死无权伤害他们。

你们要赞美主，颂扬主，感谢他，以极其谦卑的心服事他。294

§ 71. 方济各会

“温柔的亚西西的方济各，但愿他能重回人间！”

——丁尼生 (Tennyson)。

小弟兄会——fratres minores，又称米诺里特会 (Minorites)，即方济各会的正式名称——得名于亚西西的平民派米诺雷斯 (Minores)；方济各曾在一次争斗中，使他们与贵族派和解。在罗马教廷面前，方济各坚持采用这一名称，以此警戒会士不可追求显赫的地位。295 他们迅速遍布意大利，并扩展到境外；但方济各所属的一代人尚未过去，修会便已因力求保存方济各最初所立原则而引起的内部纷争，陷于分裂。没有任何其他修会的历史，能展示出这般为原则问题而在自身成员之间展开的长期冲突。这场旷日持久的争论，在中世纪论战神学中占有几乎独一无二的地位。

按照1210年的会规和方济各的遗嘱，他们应当成为一个自由的弟兄团体，致力于福音所教导的贫穷和使徒式的实践，而不是一个受严密规条约束的封闭组织。296 英诺森三世 (Innocent III.) 劝他以较早修会的会规为范本，方济各却谢绝了，仍走自己的道路。他以几处经文为根基。自1216年乌戈利诺 (Ugolino) 枢机作为保护人和顾问参与修会事务以后，一种新的影响开始显现，严格的纪律取代了方济各较为自由的组织形式。

1217年的全会会议决定派遣传教士前往意大利境外。科尔托纳的埃利亚斯曾在亚西西制作床垫，后来注定要因废置方济各最初的计划而声名狼藉；他率领一队传教士前往叙利亚。另一些人则前往德国、匈牙利、法国、西班牙和英国。作为海外传教士，方济各会士表现出无畏的进取精神，南至摩洛哥，东达北京。他们曾陪同哥伦布 (Columbus) 第二次航行前往新世界，享有这一殊荣；此后，在美洲早期的传教事业中，他们极为活跃，活动范围从佛罗里达延至加利福尼亚，又从魁北克沿圣劳伦斯河及五大湖，向南延伸至墨西哥湾。

1221年的会规缺乏统一性和明确性，显露出两种影响在起作用，一种出自乌戈利诺，一种出自方济各。其中可见数年前已经开始的斗争的痕迹。会规设立总会长，置于修会之首，并由各会院的院长组成治理机构。然而，会规仍然要求贫穷，并强调劳动的义务，免得会士成为游手好闲之徒。除为病人谋益处外，禁止出售劳动所得的产品。

1223年的会规较为简短，共有十二章，重申先前的规章，并于同年11月29日获得教皇的郑重批准。这部规章在撇开方济各的独特意愿方面又进了一步。修会的托钵性质得到大力强调；但同时也加入了服从教皇的规定，并由一位枢机担任修会的保护人和监护人。会规规定以《罗马日课经》(Roman Breviary) 为每日礼拜的用书。修道纪律取代了合乎圣经的自由。圣统制的强力干预已显而易见。1210年会规中的自由已经消失。297 彼得·迪·卡塔纳 (Peter di Catana) 被任命为修会会长，几个月后，由科尔托纳的埃利亚斯继任。方济各在最后的遗嘱中呼吁恢复弟兄团体原有的自由，反对新的体制；教皇派则竭尽所能，力图将这份呼吁彻底压制下去。

嘉勒会 (Clarisses)，即小弟兄会的女修会，得名于1255年获封圣的希菲的嘉勒 (Clara of Sciffi)；又因圣达米安教堂 (Church of St. Damian)，称为圣达米安姊妹会。方济各为她们制定了一项厉行贫穷的会规，298 并为嘉勒立了一份遗嘱，现已佚失。起初，姊妹们似乎靠亲手劳作维持生计，但不久便依从方济各的劝告，转而依靠施舍。299 会规于1219年修改，后来该会又被迫采用本笃会规。300

第三会会士 (Tertiaries)，又称悔罪弟兄姊妹会 (Brothers and Sisters of Penitence)，301 是圣方济各的第三会，嘉勒会则算作第二会；他们于1289年尼古拉四世 (Nicolas IV.) 的诏书中首次获得教皇认可。302 方济各是否曾为他们制定明确的会规，尚有疑问；但第三会在他生前已经存在，则毫无疑问。格列高利九世 (Gregory IX.) 在1228年称他们为圣方济各第三会弟兄。303 1289年的会规既为一个平信徒团体而设，也为一个会院团体而设，后者不接纳已婚者。方济各的构想包括各阶层的平信徒，男女皆可，已婚未婚均在其内。他的目的是使平信徒也能实行与修道生活相联的更高德行，并达到相应的功德等次。方济各的构想很可能取自谦卑派 (Humiliati)，即所谓伦巴第穷人——Pauperes Lombardici,；也可能取自瓦勒度派 (Waldenses)，即所谓里昂穷人，该派在方济各时代的意大利北部也颇为人知。十二世纪，谦卑派已有按照半修道规条生活的平信徒团体。1184年，他们受到卢修斯三世 (Lucius III.) 的谴责。他们似乎分为三个等级：第一是平信徒谦卑派，在日常生活中遵行特定的苦修规条；第二是住在修道院中的修士或修女；第三是教士，即共同生活的司铎。这三个等级于1201年获得英诺森三世的批准，并受到后来诸位教皇的保护，例如英诺森四世 (Innocent IV.)。304

方济各最初的计划可能是建立一个平信徒组织，后来才在心中形成修士组织的构想。1221年的全会会议正式确立了方济各会分为三个等级的制度。305 第三会最早的会规共有十三章，规定了服饰的样式、应当实行的苦修，以及其他须遵守的规条。除教皇所规定的特殊情形外，他们不得起任何誓；每年须告解三次；立遗嘱时，如有可能，应征求教区主教的意见；不得接纳被控为异端的人入会；既不得使用致命武器，也不得携带。306 已婚妇女未经丈夫同意不得入会；凡有家室者，都受命照顾家人，以此作为事奉上帝的一部分（VI. 6）。307 第三会至今仍存在于罗马天主教会中。

现在继续追述1223年以后的方济各会历史。较严格的一派力求实行方济各严守使徒式贫穷的做法，以及他在遗嘱中阐明的主张，被称为守规派 (Observants)、属灵派 (Spirituals)，或热忱派 (Zealots)。另一派主张放宽方济各的会规，并得到格列高利九世的支持；由于他们拥有自己的会院，尤其是在城市中占有较为宏伟的建筑，故常被称为住院派 (Conventuals)。308 两派轮流占据上风。教皇们反对守规派。内部纷争贯穿整个十三世纪，并延续至十四世纪很长一段时间；309 直到利奥十世 (Leo X.) 将方济各会分成两个修会，才首次把纷争压制下来，而非真正使之平息。在此期间，守规派继续力倡圣方济各的方案，其中一些人为坚持自己的原则而殉道。

方济各会内部争论的问题，是修会作为一个法人团体，是否有权以完全所有权持有财产。支持这种持有方式的教皇裁决，始于格列高利九世1230年的诏书。诏书准许修会通过按地区指定的“忠信之人”募集金钱，用以建造会院、开展传教及其他事业，并为捐赠者代为托管这些款项。英诺森四世于1245年进一步扩充了这项特权，使之包括持有书籍、工具、房屋及土地。英诺森明确区分了以完全所有权持有财产与为使用而持有财产，并授予修会为使用而持有财产的权利。这意味着修会可以接受赠与和遗赠，并以代表捐赠者的名义无限期持有。这等同于永久所有权，可以与现代的千年租约相比。英诺森还规定，修会内部一切财产的持有，均须受教皇直接监督。

守规派坚决抵制这些教皇敕令，因而遭到科尔托纳的埃利亚斯的迫害；后者大力推行教皇的政策。但守规派势力强大，1227年的全会会议罢免了埃利亚斯的总会长职务。1232年，他获准复职，却又于1239年被罢免。他转而支持腓特烈二世 (Frederick II.) 的事业，于1253年去世。

忠于方济各的一派中，有一位领袖是利奥弟兄 (Brother Leo)；他所著的《全德之镜》(Speculum Perfectionis,) 很可能是方济各的第一部传记。当在亚西西方济各遗骸安放之处兴建大教堂的计划传开时，埃利亚斯在工地放置了一个大理石容器，用来收取捐款。利奥认为这项计划亵渎了这位圣徒的身后名，便将容器砸得粉碎。由于这一举动，他在一片骚乱中被逐出了亚西西。310

有一段时期，究竟哪一派会占上风，似乎还难以断定。帕尔马的约翰 (John of Parma) 于1247至1257年担任总会长的十年间，守规派掌权；后来，他被迫辞职，退入严格隔绝尘世的修道生活。继约翰而任的是伟大的经院学者波拿文都拉 (Bonaventura)，任期为1257至1274年；总体而言，他运用自己的影响支持住院派。守规派开始与弗洛雷的约阿希姆 (Joachim of Flore) 的异梦联系起来，并将他关于一个新修会的预言应用于自己。这些见解成为不和与争斗的新源头，延续了一个多世纪。波拿文都拉谴责杰拉尔多·博尔戈 (Gerardo Borgo) 为约阿希姆著作所写的《导论》(Introduction)，以此表明反对采纳约阿希姆的见解。波拿文都拉奉1260年纳博讷 (Narbonne) 全会会议之命撰写的《圣方济各传》，于1263年的全会会议上被宣布为这位圣徒的权威传记；该书隐去了方济各的遗嘱及其他有利于守规派主张的材料，却强调修会中服从教会及纪律的因素。从此，守规派虽英勇奋战，却已无望取胜。他们无法成功对抗教廷所推行的政策。

有消息传出，说格列高利十世 (Gregory X.) 打算通过1274年里昂会议的法令，强迫修会持有财产；这使反对之火骤然燃起，一些守规派人士被投入监狱，其中包括颇有影响的作者安杰洛·克拉雷诺 (Angelo Clareno)。尼古拉三世 (Nicholas III.) 在1279年的《撒种的人出去》(Exiit qui seminat) 诏书中，311 再次明确区分了以完全所有权拥有财产与为使用而持有财产，并确认了后一种权利。他坚持一项原则，即教皇才是修会财产的最终所有者。诏书明文废除了圣方济各禁止修会向教皇谋求特权的禁令。方济各会总会长博纳格拉蒂亚 (Bonagratia) 及其后两任继任者接受了这道诏书；但凭借著述获得广泛影响的彼得·奥利维 (Peter Olivi，卒于1298年) 却强烈反对。塞莱斯廷五世 (Coelestin V.) 为弥合分裂，邀请守规派加入他所创立的塞莱斯廷隐修会；已获释出狱的安杰洛·克拉雷诺便采取了这条途径。奥利维及守规派传道人卡萨莱的乌贝尔蒂诺 (Ubertino da Casale) 则反对这样做；312 后者卒于1330年以后，虽历经重重迫害，仍忠于方济各最初的原则。

就这样，方济各诞生的那个世纪结束时，这场争论仍在继续，其激烈程度丝毫未减。到了十四世纪，争论又呈现出一些新的面貌。此时，一个教义问题被推到了前台：基督及其使徒是否实行了绝对的贫穷。1323年，约翰二十二世 (John XXII.) 试图彻底终止纷争，以教皇权威确认他们并未实行绝对贫穷这一说法。如此一来，严格的方济各会规赖以建立的根基便被撤去了。

方济各会在另一方面也背离了创立者的心意。方济各轻视学问。1220年，他因法学博士彼得罗·斯塔恰 (Pietro Staccia) 在博洛尼亚 (Bologna) 创办方济各会学校，先是斥责他，继而咒诅他。据传，他听说一位著名博士加入修会时，曾说：“我怕这样的博士会毁掉我的葡萄园。真正的博士，是那些以智慧的温柔显出善行、使邻舍得益的人。”方济各曾致信帕多瓦的安东尼 (Anthony of Padua)——这封信的真实性并无争议——说：“我同意你继续向弟兄们讲授神学，只要这种学问不熄灭他们谦卑和祷告的心。”313 然而，方济各的追随者背离了他的教导，顺应了那个非凡的十三世纪的潮流，在会院中开办学校；那个世纪尚未过半，他们就在各主要的大学学术中心牢牢立足了。1255年的一道命令要求出外传教的方济各会士学习希腊语、阿拉伯语、希伯来语及其他语言。

修会迅速扩展，从巴勒斯坦 (Palestine) 直到爱尔兰 (Ireland)。314 将它传入法国的是帕西菲科 (Pacifico) 和法国国王的姻亲博若莱的吉夏尔 (Guichard of Beaujolais)。1221年，施派尔的凯撒 (Caesar of Spires) 首次成功地在德国建立了分支；他是在前往叙利亚的途中，因科尔托纳的埃利亚斯 (Elias of Cortona) 而归信的。与他同行的有十二名司铎和十三名平信徒，其中有切拉诺的托马斯 (Thomas of Celano) 和贾诺的约尔丹 (Jordan of Giano)；我们所知的事实便依据后者的记述。这群人在特伦托 (Trent) 分手，在奥格斯堡 (Augsburg) 重聚，随后再次分散，沿莱茵河及德国其他地区开展传教工作。他们在美因茨 (Mainz)、沃尔姆斯 (Worms)、施派尔 (Spires) 和科隆 (Cologne) 建立会院，这些会院于1522年合为一个监护区。次年，德国又增设了四个监护区。315 施派尔的凯撒是修会在德国热情如火的使徒，属于守规派；他遭受严酷迫害，最终在狱中被处死。

至于英国，九名方济各会士于1224年在多佛 (Dover) 登陆，其中四名是教士，而这四人中只有一名领受了司铎圣职。他们先到坎特伯雷 (Canterbury)，再到伦敦。同时代的埃克尔斯顿的托马斯 (Thomas of Eccleston) 所写的他们早期在英国劳作的记述，316 是中世纪英国史事中最生动、最引人入胜的记述之一。在坎特伯雷，费康 (Feskamp) 的修士接待了他们；在伦敦，接待他们的则是黑衣修士。他们在牛津受到热烈欢迎。格罗斯泰斯特 (Grosseteste) 以“那坐在黑暗里的百姓看见了大光”为题讲道，宣告他们的到来。这仿佛开启了一个宗教新时代的大门。论到他们在牛津圣埃布教区 (St. Ebbe’s parish) 的定居，有人说：“那里撒下了一粒芥菜种，长起来比一切树木都大。”不久，他们便在剑桥 (Cambridge)、诺里奇 (Norwich)、北安普敦 (Northampton)、雅茅斯 (Yarmouth) 及其他中心城市立足。他们是英国前所未见的面向民众的传道人，也是最早将慈善付诸实践的人。317 当时英国乡村和城镇的状况极为悲惨。包括麻风病在内的皮肤病流行得十分可怕。致命的疫病传播极快。人们全然不知卫生预防措施为何物。到处都是死水池和成堆的垃圾。318

这些热忱的修道者，部分出于需要，部分完全出于自愿，选择在城镇中最贫穷、最受忽视的地段居住。在诺里奇，他们定居于一片沼泽，城中的污水从那里流过。在如今已属伦敦一部分的纽盖特 (Newgate)，他们栖身于臭巷 (Stinking Lane)。在剑桥，他们住进了破败的监狱。

这样的热忱得到认可，实不足为奇。民众很快就学会尊敬这些新的使徒。亚当·马什 (Adam Marsh) 加入了他们的行列；他与当时英国最有影响力的教会人士格罗斯泰斯特，一同在牛津的方济各会学校讲学。伦敦及其他城镇的市民向他们捐赠土地，国王也在什鲁斯伯里 (Shrewsbury) 赐给他们土地。到1256年，英国的会士已增至1242人，分居于四十九处。85319 方济各会士也推动了学术发展；他们兴办学校，例如在牛津，罗伯特·格罗斯泰斯特 (Robert Grosseteste) 就为他们授课。英国伟大的经院学者大多属于方济各会。埃克尔斯顿描述了早期英国方济各会士敬虔的生活、节制的习惯及欢快的性情。320 他们来到英国还不到五十年，其中一人罗伯特·基尔沃德比 (Robert Kilwarby) 就已登上坎特伯雷大主教之位；另一位方济各会士波拿文都拉 (Bonaventura) 则获提名出任约克 (York) 主教，但他辞谢了。

随着时间推移，方济各会的历史也循着人间兴盛的通常轨迹发展。321 他们离弃了起初的光景。荣誉和土地随之而来的，是道德败坏。他们因替教皇征收收入而声名狼藉。早在1235年，马修·帕里斯 (Matthew Paris) 就已斥责他们的傲慢；他还说，英诺森四世 (Innocent IV.) 把他们从得人的渔夫变成了捞钱的渔夫。亨利八世 (Henry VIII.) 没收修道院时，伦敦的基督堂 (Christ’s Church) 方济各会院于1532年首先遭殃。322

§ 72. 圣多米尼克与多米尼克会

文献——多米尼克的继任会长约尔丹 (Jordanus) 所作的最早《传记》：de principiis ordinis praedicatorum（《宣道会的起源》），收入凯蒂夫—埃沙尔 (Quétif-Echard) 的著作，其中还收有其他五部早期传记（特伦托的巴托洛缪〔Bartholomew of Trent〕，1244–1251年；洪贝尔·德·罗马尼斯〔Humbert de Romanis〕，1250年等）；另有 J. J. 贝尔蒂埃 (J. J. Berthier) 编本，瑞士弗里堡 (Freib., i. Schw.)，1892年。——H. D. 拉科代尔 (H. D. Lacordaire，卒于1861年)：《圣多米尼克传》(Vie de S. Dominique)，巴黎，1840年，第8版，1882年。另有《圣多米尼克修会史研究，1170–1221年》(Hist. Studies of the Order of S. Dom. 1170–1221)，英译本，纽约，1869年。——E. 卡罗 (E. Caro)：《圣多米尼克与多米尼克会士》(S. Dom. et les Dominicains)，巴黎，1853年。——A. T. 德雷恩 (A. T. Drane)：《宣道修士会创立者圣多米尼克史》(Hist. of St. Dom., Founder of the Friar Preachers)，伦敦，1891年。——巴尔姆与勒莱迪耶 (Balme et Lelaidier)：《圣多米尼克文书汇编，或文献史》(Cartulaire ou hist. diplomatique de S. Dom.)，巴黎，1892年。——J. 吉罗 (J. Praed.)，8卷，罗马，1737年及后。——马马基 (Mamachi)：《宣道会编年史》(Annal. ord. Praed.)，罗马，1756年。——《宣道修士会历史文献》(Monumenta ord. fratrum Praed. hist.)，B. M. 赖歇特 (B. M. Lescher)：《圣多米尼克与玫瑰经》(St. Dominic and the Rosary)，伦敦，1902年。——H. 霍尔察普费尔 (H. Holzapfel)：《圣多米尼克与玫瑰经》(S. Dom. und der Rosenkranz)，慕尼黑，1903年。

西班牙人多米尼克 (Dominic) 创立了宣道会，通常称为多米尼克会 (Dominicans)。323 他缺少阿西西那位圣徒亲切可人的气质，其生涯也很少具有与那位同时代人相应的传奇色彩。多米尼克意志坚定，热心传播正统信仰，献身于教会及其圣统。他的影响是通过自己创立的组织发挥的，而不是通过个人经历及与同时代人的接触。这也解释了为何他的传记寥寥无几，而方济各的传记却为数众多。

多明戈 (Domingo)，或称多米尼克，1170年生于西班牙的卡拉罗加 (Calaroga)，1121年8月6日卒于博洛尼亚 (Bologna)。324 他的母亲阿萨的胡安娜 (Juana of Aza)，在多米尼克会的礼仪中被尊为圣徒。七岁时，他开始随一位担任司铎的叔父接受教导。此后，他在帕伦西亚 (Palencia) 用了十年时间研习哲学和神学，据说学业出众。约1195年，他被任命为奥斯马 (Osma) 的教士；他出生所在的主教教区便以该城命名。1203年，他陪同主教迭戈·达塞韦达 (Diego d’Azeveda) 出使法国，325 为卡斯蒂利亚的阿方索八世 (Alfonzo VIII. of Castile) 之子求婚。这次以及后来数次越过比利牛斯山 (Pyrenees) 的旅程，使他接触到阿尔比派 (Albigenses)，以及英诺森三世 (Innocent III.) 派往法国南部、采取措施压制异端的使节。多米尼克投身于压制异端的运动，开始巡回布道。在图卢兹 (Toulouse) 教区的普鲁耶 (Prouille)，他建立了一所少女收容所，以抗衡阿尔比派为培养贫困贵族之女而设立的学校。他与西蒙·德·孟福尔 (Simon de Montfort) 关系密切；不过，就目前所知，除担任属灵顾问外，他并未积极参与讨伐阿尔比派的十字军。326 他试图建立一个使异端归信的传教团，得到图卢兹主教富尔克 (Fulke) 的支持；1215年，富尔克将本教区什一税的六分之一拨给他。最早与多米尼克联合的人中，有图卢兹市民彼得·切拉尼 (Peter Cellani)，他捐给多米尼克一所房屋。

多米尼克生涯中的一个转折，是第四次拉特兰会议召开期间他对罗马的访问。当时他得到英诺森三世的鼓励，不过教皇拒绝同意创立新修会的提议，吩咐他采用一项现有的修道会宪。327 多米尼克选择了圣奥古斯丁律修教士的会规，328 采用奥古斯丁会士的黑色会服，并在图卢兹建立圣罗马努斯 (St. Romanus) 会院。1216年9月至1217年复活节，他再次逗留罗马。霍诺里乌斯二世 (Honorius II.) 于1216年批准了这一组织，并确认其对财物和房屋的占有。一则不可靠的传说声称，霍诺里乌斯还将教廷宫廷导师这一要职——magister palatii——授予多米尼克。该职位的历史，至多只能追溯到格列高利九世 (Gregory IX.) 之前不久。329

关于他生平的传奇记述，将这位圣徒描绘为此时不断鞭笞自己，并从事其他极严酷的苦修。人们将种种神迹，甚至使死人复活，都归于他。

1217年，多米尼克派出修士，到各地建立分院。修会很快就在巴黎、博洛尼亚和罗马这些大城市扎下根来；巴黎著名的教会法教授雷金纳德 (Reginald) 也发愿入会。1218年，多米尼克亲自在西班牙建立了两所会院，一所在马德里 (Madrid)，收女修士；另一所在塞维利亚 (Seville)，收男修士。巴黎的第一所多米尼克会院圣雅克 (St. Jacques)，使法国的多米尼克会士得名“雅各宾派” (Jacobins)，也使法国大革命期间在那里集会的党派得名“雅各派” (Jacobites)。1224年，圣雅克会院有一百二十名会士。该会具有浓厚的法国色彩，在祷告中也包括为法国国王所作的祈祷。多米尼克会士从法国进入德国。约尔丹和圣雅克会院的其他一些成员都是德国人。尽管受到主教的禁令及其他修会的反对，他们仍很快在科隆、沃尔姆斯、斯特拉斯堡 (Strassburg)、巴塞尔 (Basel) 及德国其他城市立足。330 1221年，该会传入英国，随即在牛津定居。331 伦敦的黑衣修士桥 (Blackfriars Bridge)，其名称至今仍使人想起他们在该城的大修道院。

第一次全会会议于1220年在博洛尼亚举行。多米尼克满怀热忱地在意大利北部布道。1221年8月6日，他躺在灰烬上，于博洛尼亚逝世，葬于当地的圣尼古拉斯 (St. Nicholas) 会院；这所会院有比萨的尼古拉 (Nicholas of Pisa) 和米开朗琪罗 (Michael Angelo) 的艺术作品装饰。与方济各和帕多瓦的安东尼 (Anthony of Padua) 迅速得到教皇认可相比，这位西班牙圣徒的封圣来得较迟，直到他死后十三年，即1234年7月13日才举行。332

多米尼克去世时，宣道修士已有六十所会院，分布于普罗旺斯 (Provence)、法国北部、西班牙、伦巴第 (Lombardy)、意大利、英国、德国及匈牙利各会省；每个会省每年都举行自己的会议。到1228年，除这八个会省外，又增加了波兰、丹麦、希腊及耶路撒冷四个会省。333 各会省联合组成全会会议。每个会省由会省长或会省院长主持，会院则由院长或副院长主持。他们不采用修道院长的称号和尊位。整个团体的首领是总会长。334 教廷接连授予他们种种特权，其中包括可在任何地方布道这一重要权利。335 1228年的会宪是现存最早的会宪，但不是最初的会宪。第三任总会长佩尼亚福特的雷蒙德 (Raymund de Peñaforte) 任内，曾对会宪加以修订。336

1220年的第一次全会会议将托钵乞食定为该会的规条。337 他们效法圣方济各的榜样，不仅修士个人，连修会本身也放弃了一切拥有财产的权利。然而，托钵乞食这一特点，从未像在方济各会中那样受到强调。对多米尼克会士而言，这不是良心上的问题，该会也从未因持有财产的问题而发生分裂。1477年，西克斯图四世 (Sixtus IV.) 完全免除了修会团体守贫的义务。多米尼克最后劝勉追随者，要“心存仁爱，谦卑服事，甘愿度贫穷的生活”。338 但他们似乎从未把这一训诫十分放在心上。

多米尼克不像那位阿西西人 (Assisi)，并未将体力劳动纳入修士的其他事务中。他以研习和布道代替手工劳动。多米尼克会士是最先制定明确研习规章的修道人。多米尼克在巴黎创立圣雅克会院，派遣十七名会士前往驻守时，吩咐他们“研习并布道”。在图卢兹 (Toulouse)，他们建造了供研习用的单间。339 会士必须先完成四年的哲学与神学课程，才能获准布道，340 此后还须继续研习神学三年。

布道与拯救灵魂被定为该会的主要宗旨。341 第五任总会长罗马尼斯的洪伯特 (Humbert de Romanis) 宣称，该会的目的并非研习，但研习对于布道和拯救灵魂却极其必要。另一人说，研习是为布道而设，布道是为拯救人而设，而这才是最终目的。342 未满二十五岁者，不得在会院以外布道。343 他们布道时，除食物外，不得收取金钱或其他礼物。正如文森特·费雷尔 (Vincent Ferrer) 和萨沃纳罗拉 (Savonarola) 是中世纪最著名的多米尼克会传道人，拉科代尔 (Lacordaire) 则是该会十九世纪最著名的雄辩家。多米尼克会的工作主要面向上层阶级。他们代表了各修会中的贵族成分。

在宗教裁判所的史册中，多米尼克会占有很大篇幅。多米尼克会士是最显赫、最热心的“异端邪恶的审讯官”。但丁 (Dante) 将多米尼克形容为“对朋友仁慈，对敌人严酷”，“Benigno ai suoi ed ai nimici crudo”，344 心中所想的正是这一点。

1232年，宗教裁判所的事务大多交由他们掌管。法国北部、西班牙及德国归他们负责。345 严酷的托尔克马达 (Torquemada) 就是多米尼克会士；后来为侦察和惩罚教会中的异议者而采取的残暴手段，在该会的名声上留下了不可磨灭的污点。研究历史的人，必须将这些维护正统信仰的努力视为冷酷无情，即使参与者自己可能从未这样看待这些行为。霍诺留 (Honorius) 赐给该会的徽记，是一只口衔燃烧火炬的狗：狗负责守望，火炬则照亮世界。佛罗伦萨 (Florence) 的新圣母会院 (S. Maria Novella) 中有一幅画，表现了该会后来作为异端猎手所占的地位。画中的狗披着多米尼克会的黑白两色，追逐代表异端的狐狸；教皇与皇帝坐在宝座上，周围簇拥着顾问，满意地观看这一场景。多米尼克于1220年创设“基督的军队”，正与他铲除异端的努力有关；其成员有男有女，有已婚者，也有未婚者。后来，这个团体自称悔罪弟兄姊妹会，或第三会，或圣多米尼克第三会。如同方济各第三会的情形，其中有些成员过着会院生活。

玫瑰经在多米尼克会的历史上也占有显著地位。一个不可靠的传统，将它的首次使用归于多米尼克。据这一故事所说，在讨伐阿尔比派 (Albigenses) 的十字军战争期间，马利亚向多米尼克显现，吩咐他使用玫瑰经作为使异端归信的手段。玫瑰经包括十五遍主祷文（pater nosters）和一百五十遍万福马利亚经（ave Marias），以念珠逐一计数。多米尼克会士很早就热心诵念玫瑰经，但不久，加尔默罗会士 (Carmelites) 就在首先引入玫瑰经的荣誉上与他们竞争。声名狼藉的多米尼克会宗教裁判官兼猎巫者雅各布·施普伦格 (Jacob Sprenger)，创立了第一个玫瑰经善会。庇护五世 (Pius V.) 将1571年勒班陀 (Lepanto) 的胜利归功于诵念玫瑰经。近代的庇护九世和利奥十三世 (Leo XIII.) 都热心诵念玫瑰经。利奥在1883年9月1日的通谕中，将它的引入归于伟大的多米尼克，称它是“医治他同时代之人创伤的香膏”。这份通谕称马利亚“被置于天上权能与荣耀的最高峰……人当恳求她，借着她的代求，使她那虔敬的儿子，对于临到我们的灾难，息怒而心软”。346

利奥十三世宣布托马斯·阿奎那 (Thomas Aquinas) 为天主教神学与道德的权威导师，以及天主教学校的主保，由此给予多米尼克会最高的尊荣。

Original Text

CHAPTER VIII. THE MONASTIC ORDERS.

§ 61. The Revival of Monasticism.

Literature.—The Letters of Anselm, Bernard, Peter the Venerable, William of Thierry, Hildegard, etc.—Abaelard: Hist. calamitatum, his autobiography, Migne, 178.—Honorius of Autun: De vita claustrali, Migne, 172, 1247 sqq.—Bernard: De conversione ad clericos sermo, in Migne, 182, 853–59, and De praecepto et dispensatione, 851–953.—The Treatments of Thomas Aquinas, Duns Scotus, etc., in their Summas.—Petrus Venerablis: De miraculis, in Migne, 189. Caesar of Heisterbach (ab. 1240): Dialogus Miraculorum, ed. by J. Strange, 2 vols. Col. 1851. Excerpts in German trans. by A. Kaufmann, 2 parts, Col. 1888 sq.—Thos. à Chantimpré (d. about 1270): Bonum universale de apibus, a comparison of a convent to a beehive. Excerpts in German by A. Kaufmann, Col. 1899; Annales monastici, ed. by Luard, 5 vols. London, 1865–69.—Jacobus de Voragine: Legenda aurea, English by W. Caxton (about 1470), Temple classics ed. 7 vols. London, 1890. — William of St. Amour (d. 1272): De periculis novissorum temporum in Denifle Chartularium Univ., Paris, vol. 1.

The Lives of Anselm, Bernard, William of Thierry, Francis, Dominic, Norbert, etc.—H. Helyot (Franciscan, d. 1716): Hist. des ordres monastiques, religieux et militaires et des congrégations séculières de l’une et de l’autre sexe qui ont été établies jusqu’ àprésent, 8 vols. Paris, 1714–19; Germ. trans., 8 vols. Leip. 1753–56. He gives a long list of the older authorities.—Mrs. Jamieson: Legends of the Monastic Orders, London, 1850.—A. Butler: Lives of the Fathers, Martyrs, and Other Principal Saints, 12 vols. Dublin, 1868 sqq.—Sir William Dugdale: Monasticon anglicanum, ed. by J. Caley, etc., 8 vols. London, 1846. Based on the ed. of 1817.—T. D. Fosbroke: Brit. Monasticism, or Manners and Customs of the Monks and Nuns of England, London, 1803, 3d ed. 1845.—Montalembert: Les moins d’occident depuis St. Benoit jusqu’ à St. Bernard, Paris, 1860–77; EngI. trans., 7 vols. London, 1861 sqq.—O. T. Hill: Engl. Monasticism, Its Rise and Influence, London, 1867.—S. R. Maitland: The Dark Ages, ed. by Fred. Stokes, 5th ed., London, 1890.—Wishart: Short Hist. of Monks and Monasticism, Trenton, 1900.—E. L. Taunton: The Engl. Black Monks of St. Benedict, 2 vols. London, 1897.—A. Gasquet: Engl. Monastic Life, London, 1904, and since.—Hurter: Innocent III., vol. IV. 84–311.—J. C. Robertson: View of Europe during the Middle Ages, in introd. to his Life of Chas. V.—H. Von Eicken: Gesch. und System der mittelalterlichen Weltanschauung, Stuttgart, 1887.—A. Jessopp: The Coming of the Friars, London, no date, 7th ed., chap. Daily Life in a Med. Monastery, 113–166.—Harnack: Monasticism, Giessen, 1882, 5th ed. 1901, trans. by C. R. Gillett, N. Y., 1895.—Stephens: Hist. of the Engl. Church, chap. XIV. (Monastic Orders).—Hauck, III. 441–516, IV. 311–409.—Littledale: Monachism, ’in Enc. Brit.—Denifle: Luther und Lutherthum, Mainz, 1904 sq., draws in his treatment of monasticism, upon his great resources of mediaeval scholarship.

The glorious period of monasticism fell in the Middle Ages, and more especially in the period that is engaging our attention. The convent was the chief centre of true religion as well as of dark superstition. With all the imposing movements of the age, the absolute papacy, the Crusades, the universities, the cathedrals and scholasticism, the monk was efficiently associated. He was, with the popes, the chief promoter of the Crusades. He was among the great builders. He furnished the chief teachers to the universities and numbered in his order the profoundest of the Schoolmen. The mediaeval monks were the Puritans, the Pietists, the Methodists, the Evangelicals of their age.(1) All these classes of Christians have this in common, that they make earnest with their religion, and put it into zealous practice.

If it be compared with the monachism of the earlier period of the Church, the mediaeval institution will be found to equal it in the number of its great monks and to exceed it in useful activity. Among the distinguished Fathers of the Post-Nicene period who advocated monasticism were St. Anthony of Egypt, Athanasius, Basil, Gregory of Nyssa, Ambrose, Augustine, Jerome, and Benedict of Nursia. In the Middle Ages the list is certainly as imposing. There we have Anselm, Albertus Magnus, Bonaventura, Thomas Aquinas, and Duns Scotus among the Schoolmen, St. Bernard and Hugo de St. Victor, Eckart, and Tauler among the mystics, Hildegard and Joachim of Flore among the seers, the authors of the Dies irae and Stabat mater and Adam de St. Victor among the hymnists, Anthony of Padua, Bernardino of Siena, Berthold of Regensburg and Savonarola among the preachers, and in a class by himself, Francis d’Assisi.

Of the five epochs in the history of monasticism two belong to the Middle Ages proper.(2) The appearance of the hermit and the development of the eremite mode of life belong to the fourth century. Benedict of Nursia of the sixth century, and his well-systematized rule, mark the second epoch. The development of the Society of Jesus in the sixteenth century marks the last epoch. The two between are represented by the monastic revival, starting from the convent of Cluny as a centre in the tenth and eleventh centuries, and the rise and spread of the mendicant orders in the thirteenth century. Cluny was for a century almost the only reforming force in Western Europe till the appearance of Hildebrand on the stage, and he himself was probably trained in the mother convent. Through its offshoots and allied orders Cluny continued to be a burning centre of religious zeal for a century longer. Then, at a time of monastic declension, the mendicant orders, brought into existence by St. Francis d’Assisi and Dominic of Spain, became the chief promoters of one of the most notable religious revivals that has ever swept over Europe.

The work done by men like William of Hirschau, Bruno and Norbert in Germany, Bernard and Peter the Venerable in France, and St. Francis in Italy, cannot be ignored in any true account of the onward progress of mankind. However much we may decline to believe that monasticism is a higher form of Christian life, we must give due credit to these men, or deny to a series of centuries all progress and good whatsoever.

The times were favorable for the development of monastic communities. If our own is the age of the laic, the mediaeval period was the age of the monk. Society was unsettled and turbulent. The convent offered an asylum of rest and of meditation. Bernard calls his monks "the order of the Peaceful." Feud and war ruled without. Every baronial residence was a fortress. The convent was the scene of brotherhood and co-operation. It furnished to the age the ideal of a religious household on earth. The epitaphs of monks betray the feeling of the time, pacificus, "the peaceful"; tranquilla pace serenus, "in quiet and undisturbed repose"; fraternae pacis amicus, "friend of brotherly peace."

The circumstances are presented by Caesar of Heisterbach under which a number of monks abandoned the world, and were "converted"—that is, determined to enter a convent. Now the decision was made at a burial.(3) Now it was due to the impression made by the relation of the wonderful things which occurred in convents. This was the case with a young knight, Gerlach,(4) who listened to an abbot who was then visiting a castle, as he told his experiences within cloistral walls. Gerlach went to Paris to study, but could not get rid of the seed which had been sown in his heart, and entered upon the monastic novitiate. Sometimes the decision was made in consequence of a sermon.(5) Caesar of Heisterbach himself was "converted" by a description given by Gerard of Walberberg, abbot of Heisterbach, while they were on the way to Cologne during the troublous times of Philip of Swabia and Otto IV. Gerard described the appearance of the Virgin, her mother Anna, and St. Mary Magdalene, who descended from the mountain and revealed themselves to the monks of Clairvaux while they were engaged in the harvest, dried the perspiration from their foreheads, and cooled them by fanning. Within three months Caesar entered the convent of Heisterbach.(6)

There were in reality only two careers in the Middle Ages, the career of the knight and the career of the monk. It would be difficult to say which held out the most attractions and rewards, even for the present life. The monk himself was a soldier. The well-ordered convent offered a daily drill, exercise following exercise with the regularity of clockwork; and though the enemy was not drawn up in visible array on open field, he was a constant reality.(7) Barons, counts, princes joined the colonies of the spiritual militia, hoping thereby to work out more efficiently the problem of their salvation and fight their conflict with the devil. The Third Lateran, 1179, bears witness to the popularity of the conventual life among the higher classes, and the tendency to restrict it to them, when it forbade the practice of receiving motley as a price of admission to the vow.(8) The monk proved to be stronger than the knight and the institution of chivalry decayed before the institution of monasticism which still survives.

By drawing to themselves the best spirits of the time, the convents became in their good days, from the tenth well into the thirteenth century, hearthstones of piety, and the chief centres of missionary and civilizing agencies. When there was little preaching, the monastic community preached the most powerful sermon, calling men’s thoughts away from riot and bloodshed to the state of brotherhood and religious reflection.(9) The motto aratro et cruce, "by the cross and the plough," stood in their case for a reality. The monk was a pioneer in the cultivation of the ground, and, after the most scientific fashion then known, taught agriculture, the culture of the vine and fish, the breeding of cattle, and the culture of wool. He built roads and the best buildings. In intellectual and artistic concerns the convent was the chief school of the times. It trained architects, painters, and sculptors. There the deep problems of theology and philosophy were studied; there manuscripts were copied, and when the universities arose, the convent furnished them with their first and their most renowned teachers. In northeastern Germany and other parts of Europe and in Asia it was the outer citadel of church profession and church activity.

So popular was the monastic life that religion seemed to be in danger of running out into monkery and society of being transformed into an aggregation of convents. The Fourth Lateran sought to counteract this tendency by forbidding the establishment of new orders.(10) But no council was ever more ignorant of the immediate future. Innocent III. was scarcely in his grave before the Dominicans and Franciscans received full papal sanction.

During the eleventh and twelfth centuries the important change was accomplished whereby all monks received priestly ordination. Before that time it was the exception for a monk to be a priest. Extreme unction and absolution had been administered in the convent by unordained monks.(11) With the development of the strict theory of sacerdotalism, these functions were forbidden to them, as by the ninth oecumenical council, 1123. The synod of Nismes, thirty years earlier, 1096, thought it answered objections to the new custom sufficiently by pointing to Gregory the Great, Gregory of Tours, and Augustine as cases of monks who had priestly ordination. On the other hand the active movement within the convents to take a larger part in the affairs of society was resisted by oecumenical councils, as, for example, the Second Lateran, 1139, which forbade monks practising as physicians or lawyers.

The monastic life was praised as the highest form of earthly existence. The convent was compared to Canaan(12) and treated as the shortest and surest road to heaven. The secular life, even the life of the secular priest, was compared to Egypt. The passage to the cloister was called conversion, and the monks converts, conversi, or the religious.(13) They reached the Christian ideal. Renouncing the vow was pronounced turning to the company of the lost, to the lion’s mouth, and to the realm of blackness and death.(14)

Bishop Otto of Freising speaks of the monks as, spending their lives like angels in heavenly purity and holiness. They live together one in heart and soul, give themselves at one signal to sleep, lift up as by one impulse their lips in prayer and their voices in reading.... They go so far, that while they are refreshing the body at table, they listen to the reading of the Scriptures.... They give up their own wills, their earthly possessions, and their parents, and, following the command of the Gospel and Christ, constantly bear their cross by mortifying the flesh, being all the while full of heavenly homesickness."(15)

The enthusiastic advocacy of the monastic life can only be explained by a desire to get relief from the turbulence of the social world and a sincere search after holiness. There is scarcely a letter of Anselm in which he does not advocate its superior advantages. It was not essential to become a monk to reach salvation, but who, he writes, "can attain to it in a safer or nobler way, he who seeks to love God alone or he who joins the love of the world with the love of God?"(16) He loses no opportunity to urge laymen to take the vow. He appeals to his kinsmen according to the flesh to become his kinsmen in the Spirit.(17)

Bernard was not at peace till he had all his brothers and his married sister within cloistral walls.

Honorius of Autun, in his tract on the cloistral life,(18) after declaring that it was instituted by the Lord himself, calls the convent a shore for those tired on the sea, a refuge for the traveller from the cold and anxieties of the world, a bed for the weary to rest on, an asylum for those fleeing from the turmoils of the state, a school for infants learning the rule of Christ, a gymnasium for those who would fight against vices, a prison career for the criminal from the broad way till he goes into the wide hall of heaven, a paradise with different trees full of fruits and the delights of Scripture.

The monastic life was the angelic life. "Are ye not already like the angels of God, having abstained from marriage," exclaimed St. Bernard, in preaching to his monks,(19) and this was the almost universal representation of the age.

Kings and princes desired to be clad in the monastic habit as they passed into the untried scenes of the future. So Frederick II., foe of the temporal claims of the papacy as he was, is said to have died in the garb of the Cistercians. So did Roger II. of Sicily, 1163, and Roger III., 1265. William of Nevers was clad in the garb of the Carthusian order before he expired. Louis VI. of France passed away stretched on ashes sprinkled in the form of a cross. So did Henry, son of Henry II. of England, expire, laid on a bed of ashes, 1184. William the Conqueror died in a priory with a bishop and abbot standing by.(20)

It was the custom in some convents, if not in all, to lay out the monks about to die on the floor, which was sometimes covered with matting. First they rapped on the death table. Waiting the approach of death, the dying often had wonderful visions of Christ, the Virgin, and the saints. The imagination at such times was very vivid, and the reports which the dying gave on returning for a moment to consciousness seem to have been generally accepted.(21)

The miraculous belonged to the monk’s daily food. He was surrounded by spirits. Visions and revelations occurred by day and by night.(22) Single devils and devils in bands were roaming about at all hours in the cloistral spaces, in the air and on foot, to deceive the unwary and to shake the faith of the vigilant. The most elaborate and respectable accounts of monks, so beset, are given by Peter the Venerable in his work on Miracles, by Caesar of Heisterbach, and Jacobus de Voragine. Caesar’s Dialogue of Miracles and Voragine’s Golden Legend are among the most entertaining storybooks ever written. They teem with legends which are accepted as true. They simply reflect the feeling of the age, which did not for a moment doubt the constant manifestation of the supernatural, especially the pranks and misdemeanors of the evil one and his emissaries.

Peter the Venerable gives a graphic picture of how these restless foes pulled the bedclothes off from sleeping monks and, chuckling, carried them to a distance, how they impudently stood by, making fun while the modest monastic attended to the necessities of nature,(23) and how they threw the faithful to the ground, as at night they went about through convent precincts making "holy thefts of prayer."(24) Peter tells a good story of a poor monk who suddenly saw before him an immense demon standing at his bedside, who with difficulty bore his weight with his wings. Two others appeared at once and exclaimed to the first, "What are you doing here?" "I can do nothing," was the reply, "on account of the protection which is given by the cross and the holy water and the singing of psalms. I have labored all night and can do nothing." The two replied, "We have come from forcing a certain Gaufrid to commit adultery and the head of a monastery to fornicate with a boy, and you, idle rogue, do something, too, and cut off the foot of this monk which is hanging outside his bed." Seizing a pickaxe which was lying under the bed, the demon struck with all his might, but the monk with equal celerity drew in his foot and turned to the back side of the bed and so escaped the blow. Thereupon the demons took their departure.(25)

It is fair to suppose that many of these experiences were mere fancies of the brain growing out of attacks of indigestion or of headache, which was a common malady of convents.(26)

The assaults of the devil were especially directed to induce the monk to abandon his sacred vow. Writing to a certain Helinand, Anselm mentions the four kinds of assault he was wont to make. The first was the assault through lust of the pleasures of the world, when the novice, having recently entered the convent, began to feel the monotony of its retired life. In the second, he pushed the question why the monk had chosen that form of life rather than the life of the parish priest. In the third, he pestered him with the question why he had not put off till late in life the assumption of the vow, in the meantime having a good time, and yet in the end getting all the benefits and the reward of monkery. And last of all, the devil argued why the monk had bound himself at all by a vow, seeing it was possible to serve God just as acceptably without a vow. Anselm answered the last objection by quoting Ps. 76:11, and declaring the vow to be in itself well pleasing to God.(27)

It is unfair to any institution to base our judgment of its merits and utility upon its perversions. The ideal Benedictine and Franciscan monk, we should be glad to believe, was a man who divided his time between religious exercises and some useful work, whether it was manual labor or teaching or practical toil of some other kind. There were, no doubt, multitudes of worthy men who corresponded to this ideal. But there was another ideal, and that ideal was one from which this modern age turns away with unalloyed repugnance. The pages of Voragine and the other retailers of the conventual life are full of repulsive descriptions which were believed in their day, and presented not only a morbid view of life but a view utterly repulsive to sound morality and to the ideal. A single instance will suffice. In the curious legend of St. Brandon the Irish saint, whose wanderings on the ocean have been connected with America, we have it reported that he found an island whereon was an abbey in which twenty-four monks lived. They had come from Ireland and had been living on the island eighty years when they welcomed St. Brandon and his twelve companions. In all this time they had been served from above every week day with twelve loaves of bread, and on Sabbaths with double that number, and they had the same monotonous fare each day, bread and herbs. None of them had ever been sick. They had royal copes of cloth of gold and went in processions. They celebrated mass with lighted tapers, and they said evensong. And in all those eighty years they had never spoken to one another a single word! What an ideal that was to set up for a mortal man! Saying mass, keeping silence, going in processions with golden copes day in and day out for eighty long years, every proper instinct of nature thus buried, the gifts of God despised, and life turned into an indolent, selfish seclusion! And yet Voragine, himself an archbishop, relates that "Brandon wept for joy of their holy conversation."(28)

Gifts of lands to monastic institutions were common, especially during the Crusades. He who built a convent was looked upon as setting up a ladder to heaven.(29) Battle Abbey, or the Abbey of St. Martin of the Place of Battle, as the full name is, was built by William the Conqueror on the battle-field of Hastings and finally dedicated by Anselm, 1094. The Vale Royal in Cheshire, the last Cistercian home founded in England, was established by Edward I. in fulfilment of a vow made in time of danger by sea on his return from Palestine. He laid the first stone, 1277, and presented the home with a fragment of the true cross and other relics.

Most of the monastic houses which became famous, began with humble beginnings and a severe discipline, as Clairvaux, Citeaux, Hirschau, and the Chartreuse. The colonies were planted for the most part in lonely regions, places difficult of access, in valley or on mountain or in swamp. The Franciscans and Dominicans set a different example by going into the cities and to the haunts of population, howbeit also choosing the worst quarters. The beautiful names often assumed show the change which was expected to take place in the surroundings, such as Bright Valley or Clairvaux, Good Place or Bon Lieu, the Delights or Les Delices (near Bourges), Happy Meadow or Felix Pré, Crown of Heaven or Himmelskrone, Path to Heaven or Voie du Ciel.(30) Walter Map, writing in the last part of the twelfth century, lingers on the fair names of the Cistercian convents, which, he says, "contain in themselves a divine and prophetic element, such as House of God, Gate of Salvation," etc.(31)

With wealth came the great abbeys of stone, exhibiting the highest architecture of the day. The establishments of Citeaux, Cluny, the Grande Chartreuse, and the great houses of Great Britain were on an elaborate scale. No pains or money were spared in their erection and equipment. Stained glass, sculpture, embroidery, rich vestments, were freely used.(32) A well-ordered house had many parts,—chapel, refectory, calefactory, scriptorium for writing, locutorium for conversation, dormitory, infirmary, hospital.(33) Not a single structure, but an aggregation of buildings, was required by the larger establishments. Cluny, in 1245, was able to accommodate, at the same time, the pope, the king of France, and the emperor of Constantinople, together with their retinues. Matthew Paris says Dunfermline Abbey, Scotland, was ample enough to entertain, at the same time, three sovereigns without inconvenience the one to the other. The latest conveniences were introduced into these houses, the latest news there retailed. A convent was, upon the whole, a pretty good place to be in, from the standpoint of worldly well-being. What the modern club house is to the city, that the mediaeval convent was apt to be, so far as material appointments went. In its vaults the rich deposited their valuables. To its protection the oppressed fled for refuge. There, as at Westminster, St. Denis, and Dunfermline, kings and princes chose to be buried. And there, while living, they were often glad to sojourn, as the most notable place of comfort and ease they could find on their journeys.

The conventual establishment was intended to be a self-sufficient corporation, a sort of socialistic community doing all its own work and supplying all its own stuffs and food.(34) The altruistic principle was supposed to rule. They had their orchards and fields, and owned their own cattle. Some of them gathered honey from their own hives, had the fattest fish ponds, sheared and spun their own wool, made their own wine, and brewed their own beer. In their best days the monks set a good example of thrift. The list of minor officials in a convent was complete, from the cellarer to look after the cooking and the chamberlain to look after the dress of the brethren, to the cantor to direct the singing and the sacristan to care for the church ornaments. In the eleventh century the custom was introduced of associating lay brethren with the monasteries, so that in all particulars these institutions might be completely independent. Nor was the convent always indifferent to the poor.(35) But the tendency was for it to centre attention upon itself, rather than to seek the regeneration and prosperity of those outside its walls.

Like many other earthly ideals, the ideal of peace, virtue, and happy contentment aimed at by the convent was not reached, or, if approached in the first moments of overflowing ardor, was soon forfeited. For the method of monasticism is radically wrong. Here and there the cloister was the "audience chamber of God." But it was well understood that convent walls did not of themselves make holy. As, before, Jerome, Gregory of Nyssa, and Augustine had borne testimony to that effect, so now also did different voices. Ivo of Chartres (d. 1116) condemns the monks who were filled with the leaven of pride and boast of their ascetic practices and refers to such passages as 1 Tim. 4:8 and Rom. 14:17. The solitudes of the mountains and forests, he says, will not make men holy, who do not carry with them rest of soul, the Sabbath of the heart, and elevation of mind. Peter of Cluny wrote to a hermit that his separation from the world would not profit unless he built a strong wall against evil in his own heart, and that wall was Christ the Saviour. Without this protection, retirement to solitude, mortifications of the body, and journeyings in distant lands, instead of availing, would bring temptations yet more violent. Every mode of life, lay and clerical, monastic and eremitic, has its own temptations.

But prosperity was invariably followed by rivalry, arrogance, idleness, and low morals. If Otto of Freising gives unstinted praise to the cloistral communities, his contemporary, Anselm of Havelberg,(36) condemns the laziness and gossip of the monks within and without the convent walls. Elizabeth of Schoenau and Hildegard of Bingen, while they looked upon the monastic life as the highest form of earthly existence, saw much that was far from ideal in the lives of monks and nuns.(37) There is a chronique scandaleuse of the convents as dark and repulsive as the chronique scandaleuse of the papacy during the pornocracy, and under the last popes of the Middle Ages. In a letter to Alexander III., asking him to dissolve the abbey of Grestian, the bishop of the diocese, Arnulf, spoke of all kinds of abuses, avarice, quarrelling, murder, profligacy. William of Malmesbury,(38) writing in 1125, gives a bad picture of the monks of Canterbury. The convent of Brittany, of which Abaelard was abbot, revealed, as he reports in his autobiography, a rude and shocking state of affairs. Things got rapidly worse after the first fervor of the orders of St. Francis and Dominic was cooled. Teachers at the universities, like William of St. Amour of Paris (d. 1270), had scathing words for the monkish insolence and profligacy of his day, as will appear when we consider the mendicant orders. Did not a bishop during the Avignon captivity of the papacy declare that from personal examination he knew a convent where all the nuns had carnal intercourse with demons? The revelations of St. Bridget of Sweden (d. 1375), approved at the councils of Constance and Basel, reveal the same low condition of monastic virtue. Nicolas of Clemanges (d. 1440) wrote vigorous protests against the decay of the orders, and describes in darkest colors their waste, gluttony, idleness, and profligacy. He says a girl going into a convent might as well be regarded as an abandoned woman at once. It was true, as Caesar of Heisterbach had said in a homily several centuries before, "Religion brought riches and riches destroyed religion."(39)

The institution of monasticism, which had included the warmest piety and the highest intelligence of the Middle Ages in their period of glory, came to be, in the period of their decline, the synonym for superstition and the irreconcilable foe of human progress. And this was because there is something pernicious in the monastic method of attempting to secure holiness, and something false in its ideal of holiness. The monks crushed out the heretical sects and resented the Renaissance. Their example in the period of early fervor, adapted to encourage thrift, later promoted laziness and insolence. Once praiseworthy as educators, they became champions of obscurantism and ignorance. Chaucer’s prior, who went on the pilgrimage to the tomb of Thomas à Becket, is a familiar illustration of the popular opinion of the monks in England in the fourteenth century: —

"He was a lord full fat and in good point;

His eyen stepe and rolling in his head

That stemed as a fornice of a led;

His botes souple, his hors in gret estat,

Now certainly he was a sayre prelat.

He was not pale as a forpined gost;

A fat swan loved he best of any rost;

His palfrey was as broune as is a bery."

And yet it would be most unjust to forget the services which the monastery performed at certain periods in the history of mediaeval Europe, or to deny the holy purpose of their founders. The hymns, the rituals, and the manuscripts prepared by mediaeval monks continue to make contribution to our body of literature and our Church services. An age like our own may congratulate itself upon its methods of Church activity, and yet acknowledge the utility of the different methods practised by the Church in another age. We study the movements of the past, not to find fault with methods which the best men of their time advocated and which are not our own, but to learn, and become, if possible, better fitted for grappling with the problems of our own time.

§ 62. Monasticism and the Papacy.

Monasticism and the papacy, representing the opposite extremes of abandonment of the world and lordship over the world, strange to say, entered into the closest alliance. The monks came to be the standing army of the popes, and were their obedient and valorous champions in the battles the popes waged with secular rulers. Some of the best popes were monastic in their training, or their habits, or both. Gregory VII. was trained in the Benedictine convent on the Aventine, Victor III. proceeded from Monte Cassino, Urban II. and Pascal II. from Cluny, Adrian IV. from St. Albans. Eugenius III., the pupil of St. Bernard, continued after he was made pope to wear the shirt of the monks of Citeaux next to his body. Innocent III. wrote the ascetic work, Contempt of the World.(40)

One monastic order after the other was founded from the eleventh to the thirteenth century. The organizing instinct and a pious impulse dotted Christendom with new convents or rebuilt old ones from Mt. Carmel to northern Scotland.(41) Innocent III., after the manner in which the modern Protestant justifies the denominational distinctions of Protestantism, likened these various orders to troops clad in different kinds of armor and belonging to the same army. "Such variety, " he said, "does not imply any division of allegiance to Christ, but rather one mind under a diversity of form."(42) So Peter of Blois writing to the abbot of Eversham said, that as out of the various strings of the harp, harmony comes forth, so out of the variety of religious orders comes unity of service. One should no less expect to find unity among a number of orders than among the angels or heavenly bodies. A vineyard bears grapes both black and white. A Christian is described in Holy Writ as a cedar, a cypress, a rose, an olive tree, a palm, a terebinth, yet they form one group in the Lord’s garden.(43)

It was the shrewd wisdom of the popes to encourage the orders, and to use them to further the centralization of ecclesiastical power in Rome. Each order had its own monastic code, its own distinctive customs. These codes, as well as the orders, were authorized and confirmed by the pope, and made, immediately or more loosely, subject to his sovereign jurisdiction. The mendicant orders of Sts. Francis and Dominic were directly amenable to the Holy See. The Fourth Lateran, in forbidding the creation of new orders, was moved to do so by the desire to avoid confusion in the Church by the multiplication of different rules. It commanded all who wished to be monks to join one of the orders already existing. The orders of St. Francis and St. Dominic, founded in the face of this rule, became the most faithful adherents the papacy ever had, until the Society of Jesus arose three centuries later.

The papal favor, shown to the monastic orders, tended to weaken the authority of the bishops, and to make the papacy independent of the episcopal system. Duns Scotus went so far as to declare that, as faith is more necessary for the world than sacramental ablution in water, so the body of monks is more important than the order of prelates. The monks constitute the heart, the substance of the Church. By preaching they start new life, and they preach without money and without price. The prelates are paid.(44)

Papal privileges and exemptions were freely poured out upon the orders, especially upon the Mendicants. They were the pets of the popes. They were practically given freedom to preach and dispense the sacrament in all places and at all times, irrespective of the bishops and their jurisdiction. The constant complaints and clashing which resulted, led to endless appeals of monasteries against the decisions of bishops, which flowed in a constant stream to Rome, and gave the members of the curia a rare chance to ply their trade.(45) The convents, by their organization and wealth, and by the number of their constituents, who were free to go to Rome and spend an indefinite time there, were able to harass and to wear out the patience of their opponents, the bishops, or prolong the cases till their death.(46)

The riches, luxury,(47) and power of the great convents became proverbial. In Lorraine and other parts of Europe they were the leading influence.(48) Abbots often took precedence of bishops, just as the general chapters of the orders,(49) made up of representatives from the farthest East to the Atlantic, were more imposing than the diocesan and even the provincial councils.

A little earlier than our period the abbot of Weissenburg was able to muster as many men as his diocesan bishop of Spires, and the three abbots of Reichenau, St. Gall, and Kempten, three times as many as the bishop of the extensive diocese of Constance.(50) In the twelfth century the abbot of Fulda claimed precedence over the great archbishop of Cologne. Beginning with John XVIII. (1004–1009) the abbots were not seldom vested with the insignia of the episcopal office. The English abbots of St. Albans, Bardney, Westminster, and the heads of other English abbeys were mitred.(51) They were great personages; they sat in oecumenical councils; the bells were rung as they passed; they engaged in the hunt, had their horses and armed retinues, and entertained on an elaborate scale. The abbot of St. Albans ate from a silver plate, and even ladies of rank were invited to share the pleasures of repasts at English abbeys.

Thus, by wealth and organization and by papal favor, the monastic orders were in a position to overshadow the episcopate. Backed by the pope they bade defiance to bishops, and in turn they enabled the papacy most effectually to exercise lordship over the episcopate.

In the struggle with the heretical sects the orders were the uncompromising champions of orthodoxy, and rendered the most effective assistance to the popes in carrying out their policy of repression. In the Inquisition they were the chief agents which the papacy had. They preached crusades against the Albigenses and were prominent in the ranks of the crusaders. In the work of bloody destruction, they were often in the lead, as was Arnold of Citeaux. Everywhere from Germany to Spain the leading Inquisitors were monks.

Again, in the relentless struggle of the papacy with princes and kings, they were always to be relied upon. Here they did valiant service for the papacy, as notably in the struggle against the emperor, Frederick II., when they sowed sedition and organized revolt in Germany and other parts of his empire.

Once more, as agents to fill the papal treasury, they did efficient and welcome service to the Holy See. In this interest they were active all over Europe. The pages of English chroniclers are filled with protests against them on the score of their exactions from the people.(52) The pope treated the orders well, and in turn was well served by them. They received high favors, and they had the rare grace of showing gratitude.

The orders of this period may be grouped in five main families: the family which followed the Benedictine rule, the family which followed the so-called Augustinian rule, the Carmelites, the hermit orders of which the Carthusians were the chief, and the original mendicant orders,(53) the Franciscans and Dominicans.

§ 63. The Monks of Cluny.

Literature.—See Lit. vol. IV, pp. 367 and 861; Mabillon: Ann. ord. S. Bened., III.-V., Paris, 1706–1708; Statuta Cluniacensia, Migne, 189, 1023–47.—Bernard et Bruel: Recueil des chartes de l’abbaye de Cluni, to 1300, 6 vols. Paris, 1876–93; Consuetudines monasticae, vol. I.; Consuet. Farfenses, ed. by Albers, Stuttgart, 1900. The consuetudines are statutes and customs which convents adopted supplementary to the Rules of their orders. These of Farfa, a convent in Italy, were taken down from Odilo of Cluny and enforced at Farfa.

The Lives of St. Bernard.—C. A. Wilkens: Petrus der Ehrwürdige, Leipzig, 1857, 277 pp.—M. Kerker; Wilhelm der Selige, Abt zu Hirschau, Tübingen, 1863.—Witten: Der Selige Wilhelm, Abt von Hirschau, Bonn, 1890.—Champly: Hist. de l’abbaye de Cluny, Mâcon, 1866.—L’Huillier: Vie de Hugo, Solesmes, 1887.—K. Sackur: Die Cluniacenser bis zur Mitte des 11ten Jahrhunderts, 2 vols. Halle, 1892–94.—H. Kutter: Wilhelm von St. Thierry, ein Representant der mittelalterlichen Frömmigkeit, Giessen, 1898.—Maitland: The Dark Ages, 1890, pp. 350–491.—Hauck, vol. III.—Art. Hirschau, in Herzog, VIII. 138 sqq.

The convent of Cluny,(54) located twelve miles northwest of Mâcon, France, stood at the height of its influence in the eleventh and twelfth centuries. Founded in 910 by Duke William of Aquitaine, and directed by a succession of wise abbots, it gained an eminence, second only to that of Monte Cassino among the monasteries of the West, and became the nursery of a monastic revival which spread over Europe from the Adriatic to Scotland.

No religious locality in the Latin church enjoyed a purer fame than Cluny. Four of its abbots, Odo, Majolus, Odilo, and Hugh, attained the dignity of canonized saints. Three popes were among its monks, Gregory VII.,(55) Urban II. , and Pascal II., and the antipope Anacletus II. Gelasius II., driven from Rome, 1118, took refuge within its walls and died there lying on ashes and there was buried. The cardinals who elected Calixtus II., his successor, met at Cluny. Kings joined with popes in doing it honor.

The Cluniacs re-enforced the rule of St. Benedict in the direction of greater austerity. In Lorraine and Germany the Cluny influence began to be felt after the monastic reform, led by such men as Abbot Gerhard of Brogne in the tenth century, had run its course.(56) Such monastic leaders as William, abbot of St. Benignus at Dijon, Poppo, abbot of Stablo and Limburg, and William of Hirschau represented the Benedictine rule and were in full sympathy with Cluny. Hirschau in the Black Forest became a centre of Cluniac influence in Southern Germany and one of the chief centres of intelligence of the age.(57) Its abbot William, 1069–91, a vigorous disciplinarian and reformer, had received a thorough scholastic training at the convent of St. Emmeram, Regensburg. He was in correspondence with Anselm and visited Gregory VII. in Rome about the year 1075. The convent became a Gregorian stronghold in the controversy over the right of investiture. With the rule of Cluny before him William, in 1077, drew up a similar code for Hirschau, known as the Constitutiones Hirsaugienses, and introduced the white dress of the Cluniacs which gave rise to the sneer that the monks were cleansing their garments instead of their hearts.(58) Under William the Conqueror the Cluniacs established themselves in England at Barnstaple. William thought so well of them that he offered to one of their number, Hugh, the supervision of the religious affairs of the realm. The second house in England was the important establishment, St. Pancras at Lewes, set up by Gundrada and the Earl of Warren, the Conqueror’s son-in-law, 1077.(59) Bermondsey, Wenlock, and Thetford were other important houses. The Cluniac houses in England were called priories and their heads priors or deans.(60) Hugo, who held the position of abbot of Cluny for sixty years, 1048–1109, was the friend of Gregory VII. and during his administration Cluny was visited by Urban II., one of Hugo’s disciples, after the adjournment of the synod of Clermont. Hugo began the erection of the great basilica in 1089, which was dedicated by Innocent II. in 1131. It was the next greatest church after St. Peter’s in the West.

Under Pontius, the seventh abbot, 1109–22, the current of decay ran deep and strong. The convent had become rich in lands and goods. The plain furnishings had been discarded for rich appointments, and austerity of habits gave way to self-indulgence. Papal favors were heaped upon Pontius, and Pascal, his godfather, sent him the dalmatic.(61) Calixtus II. put his own ring on Pontius’ finger, gave him the right to exercise the prerogatives of cardinal, and the monks of Cluny the right to celebrate service with closed doors, while the interdict was in force in the diocese.

Pontius gave way completely to worldly ambition, and assumed the title of archabbot, which was the exclusive prerogative of the head of the convent of Monte Cassino. Charges were made against him by the bishop of Macon and, forced to resign, he set his face towards Jerusalem as a pilgrim. The pilgrimage did not arouse any feelings of submission, and on his return the deposed abbot made an effort to seize his former charge. He forced the convent gates and compelled the monks to swear him fealty. The sacred vessels of gold and silver were melted down and divided among the wild intruders. The devastation was then carried beyond the convent walls to the neighboring estates. The anathema was laid upon Pontius by Honorius II., and, summoned to Rome, he was thrown into prison, where he died, impenitent, 1126. This was one of the most notorious cases of monastic malversation of office in the Middle Ages.

Peter the Venerable had been elected abbot of Cluny during Pontius’ absence in the East and filled the office for nearly forty years, 1122–57. He was the friend of St. Bernard, one of the most eminent of the mediaeval monks and one of the most attractive ecclesiastical personages of his age. Born in Auvergne and trained in a Cistercian convent, he was only twenty-eight when he was made abbot. Under his administration Cluny regained its renown. In addition to the study of the Bible, Peter also encouraged the study of the classics, a course which drew upon him bitter attacks. He visited the Cluniac houses abroad in England and Spain.

On the tenth anniversary of his official primacy, Peter welcomed two hundred priors and twelve hundred and twelve members of the order at Cluny. Four hundred and sixty monks constituted the family of the mother house. No less than two thousand convents are said to have acknowledged the Cluniac rule, two of which were at Jerusalem and Mt. Tabor. In 1246 Peter introduced through a General Chapter seventy six new rules, re-enforcing and elaborating the Benedictine code already in force. (62) The use of meat was entirely forbidden except to the weak and infirm, and also the use of all confections made with honey, spices, and wine.

To the labors of abbot Peter added the activity of an author. He wrote famous tracts to persuade the Jews and Mohammedans, and against the heretic Peter de Bruys. His last work was on miracles,(63)in which many most incredible stories of the supernatural are told as having occurred in convents.

It was while this mild and wise man held office, that Abaelard knocked at Cluny for admission and by his hearty permission spent within its walls the last weary hours of his life.

During Peter’s incumbency St. Bernard made his famous attack against the self-indulgence of the Cluniacs. Robert, a young kinsman of Bernard, had transferred his allegiance from the Cistercian order to Cluny. Bernard’s request that he be given up Pontius declined to grant. What his predecessor had declined to do, Peter did. Perhaps it was not without feeling over the memory of Pontius’ action that Bernard wrote, comparing(64) the simple life at Citeaux with the laxity and luxury prevailing at Cluny.

This tract, famous in the annals of monastic controversial literature, Bernard opened by condemning the lack of spirituality among his own brethren, the Cistercians. "How can we," he exclaims, "with our bellies full of beans and our minds full of pride, condemn those who are full of meat, as if it were not better to eat on occasion a little fat, than be gorged even to belching with windy vegetables!" He then passed to an arraignment of the Cluniacs for self-indulgence in diet, small talk, and jocularity. At meals, he said, dish was added to dish and eggs were served, cooked in many forms, and more than one kind of wine was drunk at a sitting. The monks preferred to look on marble rather than to read the Scriptures. Candelabra and altar cloths were elaborate. The art and architecture were excessive. The outward ornamentations were the proof of avarice and love of show, not of a contrite and penitent heart. He had seen one of them followed by a retinue of sixty horsemen and having none of the appearance of a pastor of souls. He charged them with taking gifts of castles, villas, peasants, and slaves, and holding them against just complainants.(65) In spite of these sharp criticisms Peter remained on terms of intimacy with Bernard. He replied without recrimination, and called Bernard the shining pillar of the Church. A modification of the rule of St. Benedict, when it was prompted by love, he pronounced proper. But he and Bernard, he wrote, belonged to one Master, were the soldiers of one King, confessors of one faith. As different paths lead to the same land, so different customs and costumes, with one inspiring love, lead to the Jerusalem above, the mother of us all. Cluniacs and Cistercians should admonish one another if they discerned errors one in the other, for they were pursuing after one inheritance and following one command. He called upon himself and Bernard to remember the fine words of Augustine, "have charity, and then do what you will, "habe charitatem et fac quicquid vis.(66) What could be more admirable? Where shall we go for a finer example of Christian polemics?

After Peter’s death the glory of Cluny declined.(67) Six hundred years later, 1790, the order was dissolved by the French Government. The Hotel de Cluny, the Cluniac house in Paris, once occupied by the abbot, now serves as a museum of Mediaeval Art and Industry under the charge of the French government.(68)

The piety of Western Christendom owes a lasting debt to Cluny for the hymn "Jerusalem the Golden," taken from the de contemptu mundi written by Bernard of Cluny, a contemporary of Peter the Venerable and St. Bernard of Clairvaux.(69)

Jerusalem the Golden,

With milk and honey blest,

Beneath thy contemplation

Sink heart and voice opprest.

I know not, oh, I know not

What social joys are there,

What radiancy of glory,

What light beyond compare.

§ 64. The Cistercians.

Literature.—Exordium parvum ordinis Cisterciensiae, Migne, 166. Exordium magnum ord. Cisterc., by Conrad of Eberbach, d. 1220; Migne, 185.—Manriquez: Ann. ord. Cisterc., 4 vols. Lyons, 1642.—Mabillon: Ann. ord. St. Benedict, Paris, 1706–1708.—P. Guignard: Les monuments primitifs de la règle Cistercienne, publiés d’après les manuscripts de l’abbaye de Citeaux, Dijon, 1878, pp. cxii. 656.—Pierre le Nain: Essai de l’hist. de l’ordre de Citeaux, Paris, 1696.—J. H. Newman: The Cistercian Saints of England, London, 1844.—Franz Winter: Die Cistercienser des nord-östlichen Deutschlands bis zum Auftreten der Bettelorden, 3 vols. Gotha, 1868–1871.—L. Janauschek: Origines Cisterciensium, Vienna, 1877.—B. Albers: Untersuchungen zu den ältesten Mönchsgewohnheiten. Ein Beitrag zur Benedictinerordensregel der X-XIIten Jahrhunderte, Munich, 1905.—Sharpe: Architecture of the Cisterc., London, 1874.—Cisterc. Abbeys of Yorkshire, in "Fraser’s Mag.," September, 1876.—Dean Hodges: Fountains Abbey, The Story of a Mediaeval Monastery, London, 1904.—Deutsch: art. Cistercienser, in Herzog, IV. 116–127; art. Harding, in "Dict. Natl. Biogr.," XXIV. 333–335; the Biographies of St. Bernard. For extended Lit. see the work of Janauschek.

With the Cluniac monks the Cistercians divide the distinction of being the most numerous and most useful monastic order of the Middle Ages,(70) until the Mendicant Friars arose and distanced them both. They are Benedictines and claim the great name of St. Bernard, and for that reason are often called Bernardins in France. Two popes, Eugenius III. and Benedict XII., proceeded from the order. Europe owes it a large debt for its service among the half-barbarian peasants of Eastern France, Southern Germany, and especially in the provinces of Northeastern Germany. Its convents set an example of skilled industry in field and garden, in the training of the vine, the culture of fish, the cultivation of orchards, and in the care of cattle.(71)

The founder, Robert Molêsme, was born in Champagne, 1024, and after attempting in vain to introduce a more rigorous discipline in several Benedictine convents, retired to the woods of Molêsme and in 1098 settled with twenty companions on some swampy ground near Citeaux,(72) twelve miles from Dijon. Here Eudes, duke of Burgundy,(73) erected a building, which went at first by the name of the New Monastery, novum monasterium.

Alberic, Robert’s successor, received for the new establishment the sanction of Pascal II., and placed it under the special care of the Virgin. She is said to have appeared to him in the white dress of the order.(74)

Under the third abbot, Stephen Harding, an Englishman, known as St. Stephen, who filled the office twenty-five years (1110–1134),(75) the period of prosperity set in. In 1113 Bernard with thirty companions entered the convent, and the foundation of four houses followed, 1113–1115,—La Ferté, Potigny, Clairvaux, and Morimond,—which continued to have a rank above all the other Cistercian houses subsequently founded.

New houses followed rapidly. In 1130 there were 30 Cistercian convents, in 1168, 288. A rule was framed forbidding the erection of new establishments, but without avail, and their number in the fourteenth century had risen to 738.(76) The order, though never the recipient of such privileges as were dispensed to Cluny, was highly honored by some of the popes. Innocent III. showed them special favor, and promised them the precedence in audiences at Rome.(77)

The carta charitatis, the Rule of Love, the code of the Cistercians, dates from Harding’s administration and was confirmed by Calixtus II.—1119. It commanded the strict observance of the Benedictine Rule, but introduced a new method of organization for the whole body. In contrast to the relaxed habits of the Cluniacs, the mode of life was made austerely simple. The rule of silence was emphasized and flesh forbidden, except in the case of severe illness. The conventual menu was confined to two dishes. All unnecessary adornment of the churches was avoided, so that nothing should remain in the house of God which savored of pride or superfluity. The crosses were of wood till the statutes of 1157 allowed them to be of gold. Emphasis was placed upon manual labor as an essential part of monastic life. A novice at Clairvaux writes enthusiastically of the employment of the monks, whom he found with hoes in the gardens, forks and rakes in the meadows, sickles in the fields, and axes in the forest.(78) In some parts they became large landowners and crowded out the owners of small plats.(79) At a later period they gave themselves to copying manuscripts.(80) Their schools in Paris, Montpellier (1252), Toulouse (1281), Oxford (1282), Metz, and other places were noted, but with the exception of Bernard they developed no distinguished Schoolmen or writers as did the mendicant orders.(81) They were not given to the practice of preaching or other spiritual service among the people.(82) The general chapter, 1191, forbade preaching in the parish churches and also the administration of baptism. The order became zealous servants of the pope and foes of heresy. The abbot Arnold was a fierce leader of the Crusades against the Albigenses.

Following the practice introduced at the convent of Hirschau, the Cistercians constituted an adjunct body of laymen, or conversi.(83) They were denied the tonsure and were debarred from ever becoming monks. The Cistercian dress was at first brown and then white, whence the name Gray Monks, grisei. The brethren slept on straw in cowl and their usual day dress.

The administration of the Cistercians was an oligarchy as compared with that of the Cluniacs. The abbot of Cluny was supreme in his order, and the subordinate houses received their priors by his appointment. Among the Cistercians each convent chose its own head. At the same time the community of all the houses was insured by the observance of the Rule of 1119, and by yearly chapters, which were the ultimate arbiters of questions in dispute. The five earliest houses exercised the right of annual visitation, which was performed by their abbots over five respective groups. A General Council of twenty-five consisted of these five abbots and of four others from each of the five groups. The General Chapters were held yearly and were attended by all the abbots within a certain district. Those at remote distances attended less frequently: the abbots from Spain, every two years; from Sweden and Norway, every three years; from Scotland, Ireland, Hungary, and Greece, every four years; and from the Orient, every seven years. It became a proverb that "The gray monks were always on their feet."

The Cistercians spread over all Western Europe. The Spanish orders of Alcantara and Calatrava adopted their rule. The first Cistercian house in Italy was founded 1120 at Tiglieto, Liguria, and in Germany at Altenkamp about 1123.(84) In England the order got a foothold in 1128, when William Gifford, bishop of Winchester, founded the house of Waverley in Surrey.(85) Among the prominent English houses were, Netley near Southampton, founded by Henry III., Rivaulx, and Fountains,(86) the greatest abbey in Northern England. In 1152 there were fifty Cistercian houses in England.(87) Melrose Abbey, Scotland, also belonged to this order.

Of all the Cistercian convents, Port Royal has the most romantic history. Founded in 1204 by Mathilda de Garlande in commemoration of the safe return of her husband from the Fourth Crusade, it became in the seventeenth century a famous centre of piety and scholarship. Its association with the tenets of the Jansenists, and the attacks of Pascal upon the Jesuits, brought on its tragic downfall. The famous hospice, among the snows of St. Gotthard, is under the care of St. Bernard monks.

In the thirteenth century the power of the Cistercians yielded to the energy of the orders of St. Francis and St. Dominic.
