(58; original 593): William erected new buildings at Hirschau to accommodate the large accessions of monks and founded a scriptorum and a library. Among his writings was a work on music, de musica et tonis. Hirschau was turned into a Protestant school by Duke Christoph, 1556. Its buildings were destroyed by the army of Louis XIV. The ruins are among the most venerable monuments of Württemberg.

(59; original 594): Gundrada had visited Cluny. On her tombstone was placed the inscription Intulit ecclesiis Anglorum balsama morum, "she brought the balm of good manners to the churches of England." See Stephens, p. 254.

(60; original 595): When the monasteries were repressed by Henry VIII., there were thirty-two Cluniac houses in England. Gasquet, 218. Taunton, I. 27, speaks of thirty-eight houses and three hospitals in London belonging to the Cluniacs.

(61; original 596): The wide-sleeved over-garment stretching to the feet. The mitre, the distinctive cap of the bishop, was also frequently sent to abbots. One of the first instances was its presentation by Alexander II. to the abbot of St. Augustine of Canterbury. The abbot of Fulda received it and also the ring from Innocent II., 1137.

(62; original 597): See Migne, 189, 1026 sqq. The volume contains Peter’s works.

(63; original 598): Liber duo illustrium miraculorum. A translation of the Koran was made under Peter’s patronage. A revised edition by Bibliander was published at Basel, 1543. These works are contained in Migne, vol. 189, 507-903, which also prints Peter’s letters and sermons, and the hymns which are ascribed to him.

(64; original 599): Apologia ad Guillelmum. Migne, 182, 895-918.

(65; original 600): To this charge Peter replied that such property was much better in the hands of the monks than of wild laymen.

(66; original 601): Ep., I. 28; Migne, 189, 156. A number of Peter’s letters to Bernard are preserved, all of them laying stress upon the exercise of brotherly affection. In strange contrast to his usual gentleness, stands his sharp arraignment of the Jews. See § 77 on Missions to the Jews.

(67; original 602): The election of the abbot was taken out of the hands of the monks. During the Avignon captivity the popes, and later the French king, claimed the right to appoint that official. The Guises had the patronage of the abbey for nearly a hundred years. In 1627 Richelieu was appointed abbot.

(68; original 603): The Hotel de Cluny was a stopping place for distinguished people. There Mary, sister of Henry VIII. of England, resided during her widowhood and there James V. of Scotland was married, 1537, to Madeleine, daughter of Francis I. The municipality of Cluny purchased the abbey buildings and in part dismantled them.

(69; original 604): See Schaff, Christ in Song, and Julian, Hymnology.

(70; original 605): Cardinal Hergenröther says, "The Cistercians reached a much higher distinction than the order of Cluny." Kirchengesch., II. 351.

(71; original 606): In England they were careful breeders of horses (Giraldus Cambrensis, Speculum ecclesiae, IV. 130, and Brewer’s Preface, IV. 24) and were noted for their sheep and wool. Their wool was a popular article of royal taxation. John seized a year’s product to meet the payment of Richard’s ransom. M. Paris, Luard’s ed., II. 399. Henry III. forbade the monks to sell their wool. Henry II., 1257, taxed it heavily, etc. M. Paris, IV. 324, V. 610. See Stubbs, Const. Hist., I.541, II. 181, 200.

(72; original 607): The name comes from the stagnant pools in the neighborhood.

(73; original 608): He died on a Crusade. At his request his bones were taken back and buried at Citeaux, which became the burial place of his successors.

(74; original 609): See Helyot, V. 404. According to Hauck, IV. 337, the Cistercians were the first to introduce into Germany the exaggerated cult of the Virgin.

(75; original 610): He was a man of much administrative ability. William of Malmesbury, IV. 1, speaks of Stephen as "the original contriver of the whole scheme, the especial and celebrated ornament of our times." It is related that on a journey to Rome, and before entering Citeaux, he repeated the whole Psalter. Basil had enjoined the memorizing of the Psalter. According to the biographer of abbot Odo of Cluny, the monks of Cluny daily repeated 138 Psalms. Maitland, p. 375.

(76; original 611): Janauschek has shown that 1800, the number formerly given, is an exaggeration.

(77; original 612): Hurter, IV. 184 sqq.

(78; original 613): Peter de Roya, Ep. St. Bernard, 492; Migne, 182, 711.

(79; original 614): Hauck, IV. 336.

(80; original 615): One of the regulations of the chapter of 1134 enjoined silence in the scriptorium. In omnibus scriptoriis ubicunque ex consuetudine monachi scribunt silentium teneatur sicut in claustro. Maitland, p. 450.

(81; original 616): The Cistercians are said to have produced the first Swedish translation of the Bible. Hurter, IV. 180.

(82; original 617): St. Bernard declared that the office of the monk is not to preach, but to be an ascetic, and that the town should be to him as a prison, and solitude as paradise, quod monachus non habet docentis sed plangentis officium, quippe cui oppidum carcer esse debet et solitudo paradisus. A monk who goes out into the world, he said, turns things round and makes his solitude a prison and the town paradise. Ep., 365; Migne, 182, 570.

(83; original 618): Called at Hirschau also barbati, the bearded.

(84; original 619): See Hauck, IV. 326 sqq., for the names of the German houses.

(85; original 620): Shortly after Harding’s death, William of Malmesbury, IV. I, Rolls ed., II. 385, describes the order "as a model for all monks, a mirror to the studious, and a goad to the slothful." Gasquet, p. 221, says that three-fourths of the hundred Cistercian houses suppressed by Henry VIII. were founded in the 12th century.

(86; original 621): The ruins of Fountains Abbey in Yorkshire is described by Motley (correspondence, I. 359) as "most picturesque, and the most exquisite, and by far the most impressive ruins I have ever seen, and much more beautiful than Melrose Abbey." For the ground plan, see Dr. Venables, art. Abbey, in Enc. Brit.," I. 19, and photographs of the walls (as they are). Hodges.

(87; original 622): Stephens, Hist. of Engl. Church, p. 201.

(88; original 623): As early as 1223 such Cistercians are called fugitives by the General Chapter. Contrasting the Cistercians with the Dominicans, Matthew Paris, an. 1255, Luard’s ed., V. 529, says of them, "They do not wander through the cities and towns, but they remain quietly shut up within the walls of their domiciles, obeying their superior."

(89; original 624): Ep., 126; Migne, 182, 271.

(90; original 625): Vita prima, III. 1; Migne, 185, 303. Gaufrid, the biographer, presents an elaborate description of his qualities. He says, Bernard was magnanimus in fide, longanimis in spe, profusus in charitate, summur in humilitate, praecipuus in pietate. Alanus in Vita secunda, XVII. 47, Migne, 185, 497, gives this high praise, humanissimus in affectione, magis tamen forte in fide.

(91; original 626): This was the judgment of Philip Schaff, Literature and Poetry, p. 282. Bernard not seldom used in his letters such expressions as this, Nonne ego puer parvulus, Am I not as a little child? Ep., 365; Migne, 182, 570.

(92; original 627): The document is given in Migne, 185, 622 sq.

(93; original 628): Calvin says, Inst. IV. 2, 11, "in his de consideratione Bernard speaks as though the very truth itself were speaking." Luther, directed to Bernard by Staupitz, studied his works, and often appealed to his words. Köstlin, Life of Luther, I. 81. He praised Bernard for not having depended upon his monk’s vow, but upon the free grace of Christ for salvation. Denifle, Luther und Lutherthum, I. 56-64, tries to make out that Luther falsified when he represented Bernard as putting aside, as it were, his monastic profession as a thing meritorious. Luther, in an animated passage, declared that at the close of his life Bernard had exclaimed, tempus meum perdidi quia perdite vixi, "I have lost my time because I have lived badly, but there is one thing that consoles me, a contrite and broken heart Thou dost not despise." You see, said Luther, how Bernard hung his cowl on the hook and returned to Christ. It seems, according to Denifle, that the two clauses were not uttered at the same time by Bernard. The exclamation, "I have lost my life," was made in a sermon on the Canticles, Migne, 183, 867, and the other part was said by Bernard in a time of severe sickness. This is not the place to take up Denifle’s charge that Luther was playing fast and loose with Bernard’s ut-terances to make out a case, but it is sufficient to say that Luther was inten-ding to emphasize that Bernard depended solely upon grace for salvation, and this position is justified by expressions enough in Bernard’s writings.

(94; original 629): Her piety is greatly praised by contemporaries. The abbot of St. Benignus at Dijon begged her body for his convent. William of St. Thierry said of her that "she ruled her household in the fear of God, was urgent in works of mercy, and brought up her sons in all obedience," enutriens filios in omni disciplina. Vita prima, I. 1.

(95; original 630): Migne, 185, 260.

(96; original 631): Virtus vehementius in infirmitate ejus refulgens, etc. Vita prima, VIII. 41; Migne, 185, 251.

(97; original 632): To an Englishman, Henry Murdoch, Ep., 106; Migne, 182, 242. Aliquid amplius invenies in silvis quam in libris. Ligna et lapides docebunt te, quod a magistris audire non possis. An non putas posse te sugere mel de petra oleumque de saxo durissimo? etc. The words remind us of Shakespeare’s oft-quoted lines:

books in the running brooks,

Sermons in stones, and good in everything.

(98; original 633): Vita prima, III. 2; Migne, 185, 306. A mediaeval description of the beauties of nature is a rare thing. The Canticle of the Sun, by Francis d’Assisi, is an exception. Otto of Freising accompanied Frederick Barbarossa on his journey to Rome to receive the imperial crown, and speaks with much enthusiasm about the military display of the Germans, but had not a word to say about the glories of Rome or its monuments. See Fisher, Med. Empire, II. 229.

(99; original 634): Vita prima, I. 5.

(100; original 635): Apud vallem quae prius dicebatur vallis absinthialis et amara, coeperunt montes stillare dulcedinem, etc. Vita prima, XIII. 61; Migne, 185, 260. See also Alanus, Vita secunda, VI. 18.

(101; original 636): His letters include long compositions abounding in allegory and moralizations and brief pithy statements, which approach the subject in hand with modern directness. Alanus gives a list of churchmen high in position going forth from Clairvaux. Vita secunda, XX. 54; Migne, 185, 154

(102; original 637): Vacandard, vol. II., Appendix, gives a list of sixty-eight convents founded by Bernard.

(103; original 638): William was born at Liège about 1085, and died about 1149. In 1119 he was made abbot of the Cistercian convent of Thierry near Rheims. We meet him frequently in the company of Bernard, and in the controversies over Abaelard and Gilbert of Poitiers.

(104; original 639): Vita prima, I. 7; Migne, 182, 268.

(105; original 640): The genuineness of the letter is questionable. Ep., 492; Migne, 182, 706-713.

(106; original 641): Ep., 142; Migne, 182, 297.

(107; original 642): Si despicit frater meus carnem meam, ne despiciat servus Dei animam meam. Veniat, proecipiat, quicquid praecperit, facere parata sum. Vita secunda, VII. 22; Migne, 185, 482. Was ever sister’s appeal more tender?

(108; original 643): De consideratione, II. 1; Migne, 182, 743.

(109; original 644): Bernard refers to this election in a letter to Eugenius, Ep., 256. "Who am I," he writes, "to establish camps and march at the head of armed men?"

(110; original 645): It was on this journey that St. Bernard performed the miracle which has a humorous side. While he was crossing the Alps, the devil broke one of his carriage wheels. Bernard repaired the damage by commanding the devil to take the place of the broken wheel, which he did, and the wagon moved on again to the traveller’s comfort.

(111; original 646): Vita prima, II. 7, 45; Migne, 185, 294 sq.

(112; original 647): Migne, 182, 727-808.

(113; original 648): "Une sorte d’examen de conscience d’un pape." Vie de S. Bernard, II. 454.

(114; original 649): Bernard’s view of the functions of the papacy is given in the chapter on the Papacy.

(115; original 650): Bindseil, Colloquia, III. 134.

(116; original 651): Deutsch, Herzog, II. 634, says Er besass eine Bibelerkenntniss wie wenige.

(117; original 652): For translation see Morison, p. 227 sqq., who calls it, "among funeral sermons assuredly one of the most remarkable on record."

(118; original 653): See Dr. Storrs’s description, p. 461 sqq.

(119; original 654): Storrs, p. 388.

(120; original 655): Vita prima, III. 13; Migne, 185, 306,

(121; original 656): I. 24, Wright’s ed., p. 20.

(122; original 657): Ego mihi nec perfectionis conscius sum nec fictionis. Vita prima, III. 7; Migne, 185, 314 sq

(123; original 658): Vita prima, I. 13; Migne, 185, 262.

(124; original 659): Ep., 242; Migne, 182, 436.

(125; original 660): Verecundia, de consid. II. 1; Migne, 185, 744. The word used here is signa. See also Vita prima, I. 9; Migne, 185, 252.

(126; original 661): William of St. Thierry, in Vita prima, I. 9; Migne, 186, 253.

(127; original 662): Febricitantibus multis sanctus manus imponens et aquam benedictam porrigens ad bibendum, sanitatem o btinuit, etc., Migne, 185, 278.

(128; original 663): The only case I have found which was not a case of healing in Bernard’s miracles occurred at the dedication of the church of Foigny, where the congregation was pestered by swarms of flies. Bernard pronounced the words of excommunication against them and the next morning they were found dead and people shovelled them out with spades.

(129; original 664): Vita prima, VI.; Migne, 185, 374 sqq.

(130; original 665): Vita prima, IV. 5 sqq.; Migne, 185, 338-359. See Morison’s remarks, 372 sqq.

(131; original 666): A strange story is told of Bernard’s throwing dice with a gambler. The stake was Bernard’s horse or the gambler’s soul. Bernard entered into the proposition heartily and won. The gambler is said to have led a saintly life thereafter. Gesta Romanorum, Engl. trans. by Swan, p. 317.

(132; original 667): Life of Bernard, p. 66. Dr. Morison died 1905.

(133; original 668): Der Heilige Bernhard, I. 135-141; II. 92-95. See also Neander’s Ch. Hist, Engl. trans. IV. 256 sq.

(134; original 669): "When such works," Neander says in his history, "appear in connection with a governing Christian temper actuated by the spirit of love, they may perhaps be properly regarded as solitary workings of that higher power of life which Christ introduced into human nature." These words are adopted by Dr. Storrs, who says "it cannot be doubted that a most extraordinary force operated through Bernard on those who sought his assistance." Life of Bernard, p. 199 sq.

(135; original 670): De gratia et libero arbitrio.

(136; original 671): Ep., 174; Migne, 182, 332.

(137; original 672): De baptismo aliisque questionibus.

(138; original 673): See chapter on Mysticism.

(139; original 674): Domus ipsa incutiebat reverentiam sui ac si ingrederer ad altare Dei, Vita prima, VII. 33; Migne, 185, 246.

(140; original 675): Concordia, V. 38. See Schott, Die Gedanken des Abtes Joachim, Brieger’s Zeitschrift, 1902, 171.

(141; original 676): Hildegard’s Works, Ep., 29; Migne, 197, 189.

(142; original 677): Morison, p. 242.

(143; original 678): Mortuus vivere et vivens mortuus putabatur, Vita St. Malachy, XXXI. 74; Migne, 185, 1116. Tender as he is to his Irish friend, Bernard described the Irish people as utter barbarians in that age.

(144; original 679): Herzog, II. 634.

(145; original 680): Dogmengeschichte, III. 301.

(146; original 681): Bindseil, Colloquia, III. 152. Bernhardus hat den Jesus so lieb als einer sein mag.

(147; original 682): Vita secunda, XVII.; Migne, 185, 498.

(148; original 683): See art. Augustiner, in Herzog, II. 254 sqq., and in Wetzer-Welte, I. 1655 sqq. Theod. Kolde, D. deutsche Augustiner Congregation und Joh. von Staupitz, Gotha, 1879.

(149; original 684): At Campell, near Paris, there were not less than fifty priests, whose number was reduced by Innocent III. to twenty-two. See Hurter, III. 375. The terms canonicus saecularis and regularis do not occur before the twelfth century. Up to that time they were known as clerici religiosi, clerici regulares, clerici professi, clerici communiter viventes, etc. So Denifle, Archiv für Lit. und Kirchengeschichte for 1886, p. 174. He quotes Amort, Vetus disciplina canonicorum regul et saecul., Venice, 1747, I. 333.

(150; original 685): Chrodegana provided a common table for the clergy of his chapter, and a common dormitory. The Roman synods of 1059, 1063, recommended priests to have their revenues in common.

(151; original 686): The tradition runs that this rule was prescribed by Innocent II., 1139, for all canons regular. Helyot, II. 21.

(152; original 687): In a bull, Dec. 16, 1243, Innocent speaks of the regula S. Augustini et ordo. See Potthast, p. 954. The most distinguished convent of regular canons in France was the convent of St. Victor.

(153; original 688): The cathedral of Bristol is built up from the old abbey of St. Augustine. The Augustinian, or Austin, canons were also called the Black Canons in England. They were very popular there. St. Botolph’s, Colchester, their first English house, was established about 1100. At the suppression of the monasteries there were one hundred and seventy houses in England, and a much larger number in Ireland. Gasquet, p. 225. See W. G. D. Fletcher, The Blackfriars in Oxford.

(154; original 689): See Hurter, III. 238.

(155; original 690): In England they had thirty-two friaries at the time of the dissolution. Gasquet, 241.

(156; original 691): The Gilbertines, founded by St. Gilbert, rector of Sandringham, about 1140, were confined to England. There were twenty-six houses at the time of the suppression of the monasteries. The convents for men and women used a common church.

(157; original 692): Norbert’s Works and Life are given in Migne, vol. 170, and his Life in Mon. Ger. XII., 670 sqq.; Germ. trans. by Hertel, in Geschichtschreiber der deutschen Vorzeit, Leipzig, 1881. See also Hauck, IV. 350-66; J. von Walter, Die ersten Wanderprediger Frankreichs, vol. II. Leipzig, 1906, pp. 119-129, and the art. Praemonstratenser, X. 267 sqq., and Norbert, IX. 448 sqq., in Wetzer-Welte, and Praemonstratenser, in Herzog, XV. 606 sqq., and the literature there given; and Gasquet, The Engl. Praemonstratensians, in transactions of the Royal Hist. Soc., vol. XVII. London, 1903.

(158; original 693): Walter puts Norbert in the group of the itinerant preachers of the age.

(159; original 694): Pratum monstratum.

(160; original 695): Hurter, IV. 206.

(161; original 696): Bernard, Sermon, XXII.; Ep., 56

(162; original 697): See Hurter, IV. 208.

(163; original 698): In England there were more than thirty Premonstrant convents at the suppression of the monasteries. Bayham and Easley are their best preserved abbeys.

(164; original 699): Consuetudines Carthusienses, printed among Bruno’s Works in Migne, 153, 651-759. Peter Dorland, Chronicon Carthusianae, Col. 1608. For literature see Wetzer-Welte, art. Karthäuser, VII. 203, and the art. Bruno, vol. II. 1356-63. Bruno’s Works in Migne, 152, 153. In his Com. on the Romans he anticipates Luther by inserting sola, "alone" in Rom. 3:28, "a man is justified by faith alone, without the works of the law." See Dr. Fr. Duesterdieck, Studien u. Kritiken, 1903, p. 506.

(165; original 700): The device of the order is a globe surmounted by a lion with the motto Stat crux dum volvitur orbis, "The cross stands while the globe turns."

(166; original 701): The following legend was invented to account for Bruno’s decision. In 1082 he was present at the mortuary services over Raymond, canon of Notre Dame, Paris. When the words were said, "Quantas habes iniquitates et peccata?""how many sins and iniquities hast thou?" the dead man rose up and replied, "justo dei judicio accusatus sum," "I am accused by the just judgment of God." The next day at the repetition of the words, the dead rose again and exclaimed, "justo dei judicio judicatus sum," "I am judged by the just judgment of God." The third day the dead man rose for the third time and cried out, "justo dei judicio condemnatus sum," "I am condemned by the just judgment of God." This incident was inserted into the Roman Breviary, but removed by order of Urban VIII., 1631. Hergenröther says the legend is still defended by the Carthusians. Kirchengesch., II. 353.

(167; original 702): Peter the Venerable says of a visit to Chartreuse, Ep., VI. 24, inaccessibiles pene nivibus et glacie altissimas rupes non abhorrui, "I shrank not back from the high rocks made inaccessible by snow and ice." Hurter’s description, IV. 150, makes the location attractive.

(168; original 703): Nova collectio statutorum Ord. Carthusiensis, Paris, 1682.

(169; original 704): For the plan of a Carthusian monastery, see Dr. Venables’ art. Abbey, in "Enc. Brit.," I. 20 sq.

(170; original 705): Vestes vilissimas ac super omne religionis propositum abjectissimas ipsoque visu horrendas assumpserunt. Pet. Ven., De miraculis, II. 28.

(171; original 706): A movement among the Carthusians to pass over into other orders, where the discipline was less rigid, was severely rebuked by Innocent III. Hurter, IV. 161.

(172; original 707): Medicinis, excepto cauterio et sanguinis minutione perraro utimur, quoted by Hurter, IV. 154, from the Constitutions of Guigo. Bleeding for medicinal purposes seems to have been common in convents. It was practised in the convent of Heisterbach, Caesar of Heisterbach, Dial., XI. 2. According to the life of Bernard of Thiron, it was the custom in some convents for monks suffering from headache or other physical ailments to have the abbot place his hands on their bodies, trusting to his miraculous power for healing. See Walter, Die ersten Wanderprediger Frankreichs, Leipzig, 1906, II. p. 50.

(173; original 708): And yet they have furnished at least four cardinals, seventy archbishops and bishops, and have had rich churches noted for their works of art like the one in Naples, or the church at Pavia, where lapis lazuli is freely used. See Hurter, IV. 158.

(174; original 709): Pet. Ven., Epp., I. 24, IV. 38. Peter gives a list of the books he sent.

(175; original 710): "The discipline was too rigid, the loneliness too dreadful for our tastes and climate." Jessopp, The Coming of the Friars, p. 125.

(176; original 711): The order was suppressed in France at the time of the Revolution. The monks, however, were permitted to return to Grand Chartreuse in 1816, paying a rental of 3000 francs to the government. The mother convent has again been broken up by the Associations Law of 1903. There were at that time one hundred and fifty monks in the house. Some of them went to Piedmont, and others to Tarragona, Spain, where they have set up a distillery for their precious liqueur.

(177; original 712): Ordo B. M. V. de Monte Carmelo is the name given by Innocent IV. The brethren are called fratres eremiti de monte Carmelo, by Honorius III., in his sanction of the order, 1226. The art. Carmelite, in Wetzer-Welte, II. 1966-1976, and Karmeliter, in Herzog, X. 84-88, give a good account and contain lists of literature. Potthast, I. No. 7524.

(178; original 713): The convent on Mt. Carmel is a conspicuous object as you approach the coast from the Mediterranean, and from the hills round about Nazareth. The present building was erected in 1828, and is an hour’s walk from Haifa. Napoleon used the former buildings for a hospital during his Syrian campaign.

(179; original 714): Speculum Carmelitarum seu historia Eliani ordinis, 4 vols. Antwerp, 1680.

(180; original 715): Benedict XIII., in 1725, gave quasi-sanction to the order’s claim by permitting it to erect a statue to Elijah in St. Peter’s. It bears the inscription Universus ordo Carmelitarum fundatori suo St. Eliae prophetae erexit.

(181; original 716): The Carmelites are often called the Brotherhood of the Scapulary. The scapulary is a sleeveless jacket covering the breast and back, and was originally worn over the other garments when the monk was at work. The garment has been the frequent subject of papal decree down to Leo XIII., 1892. July 16 has been set apart since 1587 as a special festival of the scapulary, and is one of the feasts of the Virgin. A work has been written on the proper use of the scapulary, by Brocard: Recueil des instructions sur la devotion au St. Scapulaire de Notre Dame de Monte Carmelo, Gand, 4th ed. 1875. Simon Stock was one hundred when he died.

(182; original 717): Hergenröther-Kirsch, Kirchengesch., II. 362, says it is introduced as a matter of "pious opinion," fromme Meinung.

(183; original 718): The original bull has not been found, and its authenticity has been a subject of warm dispute, in the Catholic church. The pertinent words of Mary are Ego mater gratiose descendam sabbato post eorum mortem et, quot inveniam in purgatorio, liberabo. "I, mother, will graciously descend on the Sabbath after their death, and whomever I find in purgatory I will free." One ground for doubting the authenticity of the bull is that Mary promises to forgive sins. Paul V., in 1613, decreed that this "pious faith" should be preached. See art. Sabbatina, in Wetzer-Welte, X. 1444-1447

(184; original 719): By the decision of Clement VIII., 1593, the Barefoot monks became an independent order, and elect their own general superior. Hurter, IV. 213, concludes his short account of the Carmelites by saying, that among other things which they used to exaggerate to a ridiculous extent was the number of their houses, which they gave at 7500, and of their monks, which they gave as 180,000.

(185; original 720): Falco, Antonianae Hist. compendium, Lyons, 1534. Uhlhorn, D. christl. Liebesthätigkeit d. Mittelalters, Stuttg. 1884, 178-186, 343 sqq.

(186; original 721): The Antonites regarded St. Anthony as the patron of stable animals, a view popularly held in Italy. An example of this belief is given in the Life of Philip Schaff, 56 sq.

(187; original 722): The Trinitarians were also called Maturines, from their house in Paris near St. Mathurine’s chapel. They had a few houses in England. A Spanish order with the same design, the Ordo B. V. M. de Mercede redemptionis captivorum, was founded by Peter Nolasco and Raymond of Pennaforte. See Hurter, IV. 219.

(188; original 723): The last abbess died 1799. Since 1804 the abbey of Font Evraud has been used as a house for the detention of convicts. Henry II. of England and Richard Coeur de Lion were buried at Font Evraud. For the literature of the order, see Herzog, VI. 125, and J. von Walter, Die ersten Wanderprediger Frankreichs, Studien zur Gesch. des Mönchthums, Robert von Abrissel, I. Leipzig, 1903.

(189; original 724): Ut capillos non nutriant suos. Walter, Wanderprediger, II. 112.

(190; original 725): Hurter, IV. 140. See art. Grammont, in Wetzer-Welte, VI. 990 sqq.

(191; original 726): Walter, II. 143.

(192; original 727): Fratres saccati, fratres de sacco, saccophori, etc. See art. Sackbrüder, in Herzog, XVII. 327. Gasquet, 241 sq.

(193; original 728): See Coulton, p. 301.

(194; original 729): Among others who were expecting the millennium soon to dawn, was Norbert, who wrote to St. Bernard that the age in which he lived was the age of antichrist. Bernard, Ep., 56; Migne, 182, 50, wrote back taking a contrary view.

(195; original 730): The name of Héloïse was perhaps as widely known, but it was for her connection with Abelard, not for her works in the Church. The Latin form of Hildegard is Hildegardis. M. Paris, Luard’s Ed., V. 195, in his summary of the events of 1200-1250, mentions Hildegard and Elizabeth of Thuringia as the prominent religious female characters of the period, but Hildegard died 1177.

(196; original 731): Ep., XXVI. sq.; Migne, 197, 185 sq.

(197; original 732): Ep., XXII. On the other hand, Hildegard asked Bernard to pray for her.

(198; original 733): animam meam sicut flammam comburens, Migne, 197, 190. St. Bernard, writing to Hildegard, spoke of the "sweetness of her holy love," and Hildegard compares the abbot of Clairvaux to the eagle and addresses him as the most mild of fathers, mitissime pater.

(199; original 734): non visiones in somnis, nec dormiens, nec in phrenesi, nec corporeis oculis aut auribus exterioris, nec in abditis locis percepi, sed eas vigilans, circumspiciens in pura mente oculis et auribus interioris hominis, etc. Scivias, I. Praefatio, Migne, 197, 384.

(200; original 735): Scivias. See Migne, 197, 93. This is the chief collection of her visions. Migne, 197, 383-739.

(201; original 736): Ep., I.; Migne, 197, 146.

(202; original 737): Migne, 197, 117.

(203; original 738): de plantis, Migne, 197, 1139.

(204; original 739): Migne, 197, 1210.

(205; original 740): Her writings are given in Migne, 195, 119-196. First complete edition by F. W. C. Roth: Die Visionen der heiligen Elizabeth, Brünn, 1884. See Preger: Gesch. d. deutschen Mystik, 1, 37-43.

(206; original 741): Migne, 195, 146.

(207; original 742): acorpore rapta sum in exstasim, p. 135, oreram in exstasi et vidi, p. 145.

(208; original 743): Migne, 195, 146.

(209; original 744): After the convent St. Johannes in Flore, which he founded. The members of Joachim’s order are called in the papal bull, Florentii fratres, Potthast, No. 2092, vol. I. 182.

(210; original 745): When Richard Coeur de Lion was in Sicily on his way to Palestine in 1190, he was moved by Joachim’s fame to send for him. The abbot interpreted to him John’s prophecy of anti-christ, whom he declared was already born, and would in time be elevated to the Apostolic chair and strive against everything called of God. De Hoveden, Engl. trans., II. pp. 177 sqq.

(211; original 746): Joachim had set forth his views against the Lombard in a tract to which the council referred. See Mansi, xxii., and Hefele-Knöpfler, V. 880 sq.

(212; original 747): Joachim, in a list, 1200, gives these three writings and also mentions works against the Jews and on the articles of the Christian faith. Schott, p. 170, counts twenty-four works, genuine and ungenuine, which are ascribed to him. Among those pronounced ungenuine are the commentaries on Jeremiah and Isaiah which were much used by the Franciscans from the middle of the thirteenth century on. They call Rome, Babylon and show a bitter hostility to the pope, representations which are in conflict with Joachim’s genuine writings. They also abound in detailed prophecies of events which actually occur-red. "If these books were genuine," says Döllinger, p. 369, "the exact fulfilment of the many predictions would present the most wonderful phenomenon in the history of prophecy."

(213; original 748): principium, fructificatio, finis.

(214; original 749): See Denifle, pp. 53 sqq.

(215; original 750): spiritualis ecclesia, also called ecclesia contemplativa, Denifle, pp. 56 sqq.

(216; original 751): Parvuli Christi or parvuli de latina ecclesia, a name for monks.

(217; original 752): In some passages Joachim also speaks of two orders. See Döllinger, 376.

(218; original 753): So Schott, p. 180, Die Fructification ist nichts anders als ein neuer Ausdruck für den Entwicklungsgedanken.

(219; original 754): See Schott, 175.

(220; original 755): Döllinger, 379; Schott, 178, etc.

(221; original 756): The Fourth Lateran Council, Canon II.

(222; original 757): He also quotes freely from Jerome, Ambrose, Gregory the Great, and other Fathers.

(223; original 758): Introductorius in Evangelium aeternum.

(224; original 759): Or the "Gospel of the Holy Spirit." See Denifle, p. 60.

(225; original 760): The practical English monk, M. Paris, speaks of Joachim’s doctrines as "new and absurd." III. p. 206.

(226; original 761): Coulton’s Reproduction, pp. 105, 163.

(227; original 762): Ordines mendicantium..

(228; original 763): The practice of mendicancy was subsequently adopted by the Carmelites, 1245, the Augustinian friars, 1256, and several other orders. In 1274 Gregory X. abolished all mendicant orders except the Franciscans, Dominicans, Augustinian friars, and Carmelites.

(229; original 764): Wilhelm Kothe: Kirchliche Zustände Strassburgs im 14ten Jahrhundert, Freib. im Br., 1903, says the mendicant monks were distrusted in Strassburg from the beginning and the Dominicans had to remain outside of the walls till 1250, and their attempt at that time to build a chapel stirred up a warm conflict.

(230; original 765): Paradiso, canto XI. Longfellow’s trans.

(231; original 766): Harnack says: "If ever man practised what he preached, that man was Francis." Monachism, p. 68.

(232; original 767): Karl Müller accepts the evidence which Sabatier gives. See Literatur-Zeitung, 1895, p. 181.

(233; original 768): This does not mean that the Franciscans in their early period were idlers. They were expected to work. Sabatier, S. François, VIII. p. 138.

(234; original 769): nudus nudum Christum in cruce sequi, Walter, Wanderprediger.

(235; original 770): Pauperem dominum ad mortem pauper spiritu pauper sequebatur, Walter, II. 44.

(236; original 771): Leve jugum Christi per apostolorum vestigia ferre decrevit, Walter, II. 83.

(237; original 772): Walter, Wanderprediger Frankreichs, p. 168, has brought this out well.

(238; original 773): Hergenröther says, "Chivalry reappeared in them in a new form. In happy unison were blended peace and battle, contemplation and active life, faith and love, prudent moderation and flaming enthusiasm." Kirchengeschichte, II. 369.

(239; original 774): "Of one thing," says Trevelyan, "the friar was never accused. He is never taunted with living at home in his cloister and allowing souls to perish for want of food." England in the Age of Wycliffe, p. 144.

(240; original 775): So called in the bull of Gregory IX., 1228; Potthast, I. p. 703.

(241; original 776): See the quotations from the Speculum andVita secunda of Celano, in Seppelt, pp. 234 sqq. Also Sabatier, S. François, ch. XVI.

(242; original 777): For the relations of the mendicant orders with the University of Paris, see Denifle, Chartularium Univ. Parisiensis, I.; Seppelt, Der Kampf der Bettelorden an der Univ. Paris in der Mitte des 13ten Jahrh.; Felder, Gesch. der wissenschaftlichen Studien im Franciskanerorden bis c. 1250.

(243; original 778): Chartul., I. 285.

(244; original 779): Chartul., I. 309-313, gives Humbert’s long letter.

(245; original 780): Chartul., I. 381. See chapter on Universities.

(246; original 781): Villani, V. 25, says, "This vision was true, for it was evident the Church of God was falling through licentiousness and many errors, not fearing God."

(247; original 782): Bishop Creighton, Hist. Lectures, p., 112, says, "The friars were far more destructive to ecclesiastical jurisdiction than any Nonconformist body could be, at the present day, to the influence of any sensible clergyman." He is speaking of the Anglican Church.

(248; original 783): The bulls are dated March 7 and 29. See Potthast, I. 590. The same privilege was conceded to the Carmelites, April 9, 1229.

(249; original 784): Potthast, I. 697, 721.

(250; original 785): Potthast, I. 701, 706.

(251; original 786): June 10, 1228, Potthast, I. 707.

(252; original 787): See Potthast, Nos. 6508, 6542, 6654, etc.

(253; original 788): Potthast, II. 1280. Innocent died a few weeks after issuing this bull and, as is said, in answer to the prayers of the mendicants. Hence came the saying, "from the prayers of the Preachers, good Lord, deliver us." A litanis praedicatorum libera nos, Domine.

(254; original 789): In his treatise de periculis novissorum temporum, "The Perils of the Last Times," Basel, 1555, William has been held up as a precursor of Rabelais and Pascal on account of his keen satire. He was answered by Bonaventura and by Thomas Aquinas in hiscontra impugnantes religionem. Alexander IV. ordered William’s treatise burnt, and in the bull, dated Oct. 5, 1256, declared it to be "most dangerous and detestable," valde perniciosum et detestabilem. See Potthast, II. 1357. When an edition of Williaim’s treatise appeared at Paris, 1632, the Mendicants secured an order from Louis XIII. suppressing it. William was inhibited from preaching and teaching and retired to Franche-Comte, where he died. See Chartularium Univ. Parisiensis, I. Nos. 295, 296, 314, 318, 321, 332, 339, 343, 315, etc.

(255; original 790): Matthew Paris in his résumé of the chief events of 1200-1250 has this to say of the decay of the orders, "These Preachers and Minorites at first led the life of poverty and greatest sanctity and devoted themselves assiduously to preaching, confessions, divine duties in the church, reading and study, and abandoned many revenues, embracing voluntary poverty in the service of God and reserving nothing in the way of food for themselves for the morrow, but within a few years, they got themselves into excellent condition and constructed most costly houses, etc." Luard’s ed., V. 194.

(256; original 791): The former unfavorable view of most Protestant historians concerning Francis is no longer held. Hallam, Middle Ages, II. 197, called him "a harmless enthusiast, pious and sincere, but hardly of sane mind." Lea, representing the present tendency, goes far, when he says. "No human creature since Christ has more fully incarnated the ideal of Christianity than Francis." Hist. of Inquis., I. 260. Harnack says, "If ever a man practised what he preached, it was St. Francis." An anonymous writer, reviewing some of the Franciscan literature in the Independent, 1901, p. 2044, seriously pronounced the judgment that "Since the Apostles, Francis received into his being the love of Christ toward men and the lower creatures more fully than any other man, and his appearance has been an epoch of spiritual history only less significant than that of the original Good Tidings." More judicious is Sabatier’s verdict, Vie de S. Franc., p. viii., "that Francis is pre-eminently the saint of the Middle Ages. Owing nothing to the Church, he was truly theodidact."

(257; original 792): The Speculum perfectionis, pp. 94 sqq., leaves no room for doubting the gift of the church to Francis. The gift was made on condition that the chapel should always remain the centre of the brotherhood.

(258; original 793): That is, in the cell a few yards from Portiuncula. Both Portiuncula and the cell, which has been turned into a chapel, are now under the roof of the basilica.

(259; original 794): Sabatier limits the Rule to these passages of Scripture. Thomas of Celano, Vita sec., II. 10, says that Francis "used chiefly the words of the Holy Gospel" but says further that "he added a few other things which were necessary for a holy life pauca tamen inseruit alia."

(260; original 795): In case of necessity the wearing of sandals was permitted. Speculum, p. 8.

(261; original 796): Speculum, 38; 2 Cel. 3, 35. The woman was expected to sell the book.

(262; original 797): On the meaning of idiota, see Felder, p. 61, and Böhmer, p. xi. Felder, pp. 59 sqq., makes an effort to parry the charges that Francis lacked education and disparaged education for his order. Celano calls him vir idiota and says nullis fuit scientiae studiis innutritus. He also speaks of him as singing in French as he walked through a forest. See the notes in Felder.

(263; original 798): See Böhmer, pp. xiii. sq., 69 sq.

(264; original 799): Giotto has made the meeting with Innocent seated on his throne the subject of one of his frescoes. A splendid contrast indeed, the sovereign of kings and potentates and yet the successor of Peter, recognizing the humble devotee, whose fame was destined to equal his own! The date usually given is 1209. Sabatier gives reasons for the change to 1210. St. François, p. 100.

(265; original 800): M. Paris, Luard’s ed., III. 132. Sabatier remarks that the incident has a real Franciscan color and is to be regarded as having some historic basis.

(266; original 801): Spectulum, p. 49. See also Cel. 10; 2 Cel. 97. Sabatier insists that Francis had "no intention of creating a mendicant order, but a working order." S. François, p.138. Denifle also called attention to this feature, Archiv, 1885, p. 482.

(267; original 802): Speculum, xvii.

(268; original 803): Celano in his first Life speaks of the sacred intercourse between Francis and holy Poverty, commercium cum sancta paupertate. The work entitled Sacrum commercium, etc., relates in full the story accounting for Francis’ espousal of Poverty.

(269; original 804): Jacopone da Todi took up the idea and represented Poverty going through the earth and knocking at the door of convent after convent, and being turned away. Hase, with reference to Francis’ apotheosis of Poverty, says, that Diogenes was called a mad Socrates, and so Francis was a mad Christ, ein verrückter Christus. KirchenGesch. II. 382. In its opening chapter the Commercium explains the beatitude, "Blessed are the poor in spirit," to refer to the renunciation of worldly goods, and puts into the hands of Poverty the keys of the kingdom of heaven.

(270; original 805): Francis was a deacon and never a priest. According to Thomas à Celano, Francis was austere in his relations to women, and knew only two women by sight. Sabatier, pp. 169 sq., pronounces this portraiture false and speaks of "the love of St. Francis and St. Clara." Here, as in other places, the biographer allows himself the license of the idealist. Francis’ last message to Clara is given in the Speculum Perfectionis, pp. 180 sqq. The Franciscan Rule of 1223 forbids, suspicious conferences with women, "but allows the friars to enter monastaries of nuns by permission of the Holy See. See Robinson, p. 73.

(271; original 806): According to the Speculum, pp. 1-4, 76, Francis made three Rules. Sabatier defines them as the Rule of 1210, confirmed by Innocent III., the Rule of 1221, confirmed by Honorius III., which in part misrepresented Francis’ views. The Rule of 1223 went further in this direction and completely overthrew Francis’ original intention. The first clause of the Rule of 1228 runs, "Brother Francis promises obedience and reverence to the lord pope, Honorius, and his successors." This rule is still in force in the first Franciscan order. Madonnet substantially agrees with Sabatier as does Karl Müller. Father Robinson, himself a Franciscan friar, pp. 25-31, 182, following the Quaracchi editors, who are Franciscans also, denies the genuineness of the Rule of 1221, and holds that there were only two Rules, and that there is no conflict between them. This conclusion is in the face of Francis’ will and the plain statement of Leo’s Legenda which, however, Robinson pays little attention to.

(272; original 807): See Sabatier, S. François, p. 23. Peter of Catana died March 10, 1221, a year after his elevation.

(273; original 808): Almost everything done in the order after 1221 was done either "without Francis’ knowledge or against his, will and mind," are the words of Sabatier. S. François, p. 316.

(274; original 809): For the Latin text of this remarkable writing see Speculum, 309-313. Sabatier gives a French trans., in his S. Francois, 389 sqq.

(275; original 810): This designation was not original with Francis. In the fourth century Hilarion called his body the ass which ought to have chaff and not barley. Schaff, Ch. Hist. III., 190.

(276; original 811): Nouvelles Etudes d’hist. rel., 2d ed., Paris, 1844, pp. 333-35l. No reasonable doubt is possible that Francis was the author of the Canticle, now that the Speculum has been published (pp. 234 sqq., and Sabatier’s remarks, 278-288).

(277; original 812): Speculum, 223-226. See Longfellow’s poem, The Sermon of St. Francis.

(278; original 813): Little Flowers of Francis, 93-99. Anthony of Padua, also a Franciscan, according to the same authority, pp. 166 sqq., preached to the fishes at Rimini and called upon them to praise God, seeing they had been preserved in the flood and saved Jonah. The fishes ascended above the water and opened their mouths and bowed their heads. The people of the city were attracted and Anthony used the occasion to preach a powerful sermon. In the legend of St. Brandon, it is narrated that when St. Brandon sang, the fishes lay as though they slept. Aurea Legenda, Temple Classics, vol. V.

(279; original 814): Speculum, p. 241.

(280; original 815): Quoniam, gratia Spiritus sancti cooperante, ita sum unitus et conjunctus cum Domino meo quod per misericordiam suam bene possum In ipso altissimo jocundari. Speculum, p. 237.

(281; original 816): Mortem cantando suscepit. 2 Cel., 3, 139.

(282; original 817): Speculum, 244 sq.

(283; original 818): Potthast, 8236, 8240, vol. I. 709-710.

(284; original 819): There, after much searching, it is said to have been found, 1818. Plus VII., in 1822, declared it to be the genuine body of Francis.

(285; original 820): Sabatier gives a charming description of the region, showing his own intense sympathy with nature.

(286; original 821): p. 194. It is at first sight striking that the author does not give a detailed description of this wonderful event. From another standpoint the passing reference may be regarded as a stronger testimony to its reality. See Sabatier’s observations, Speculum, pp. lxvi. sqq. It will be remembered that Sabatier places this document in 1227, only seven months after Francis’ death.

(287; original 822): In three bulls, Potthast, 10307, 10308, 10309, vol. I. 875.

(288; original 823): The evidence for the genuineness is accepted by Sabatier, S. François, 401 sqq. Among other testimonies he adduces a Benediction upon Leo ostensibly written by Francis’ own hand, and found among the archives of Assisi. See Speculum, p. lxvii. sq. On the margin of this document Leo has written his authentication. He vouches for the scene on the Verna and the stigmata. If this document be genuine, as Sabatier insists, it is the most weighty of all the testimonies. Hase stated, as strongly as it can be stated, the view that the whole tale was a fraud, invented by Elias, Francis of Assisi, 143-202, and Kirchengeschichte, II. 385 sqq. Elias was the only eye-witness, and it is contrary to all laws that he should have denied the people the privilege of looking at the marks, after the saint was dead, if they had really been there. On the contrary, he hurried the body to the grave. Hase makes a strong case, but it must be remembered that he wrote without having before him the later evidence brought to light by Sabatier

(289; original 824): S. François, 401 sqq. Sabatier does not regard them as miraculous but as unusual, as, for example, are the mathematical powers and musical genius of youthful prodigies. According to Hase, this was also Tholuck’s explanation. See art. Stigmatization, in Herzog, XIV. 728-734, which takes the same view and compares the scars to the effects of parental states before childbirth.

(290; original 825): So Hausrath. The first Franciscan chronicler, Salimbene, d. 1287, no doubt expressed the feeling of his age when he said, "Never man on earth but Francis has had the five wounds of Christ." The Dominicans claimed the stigmata for St. Catherine of Siena, but Sixtus IV., in 1475, prohibited her being represented with them.

(291; original 826): Bonaventura’s legendary Life makes Francis a witness to the stigmata, but he evidently is seeking to establish the fact against doubts.

(292; original 827): In his will he refers again and again to his divine appointment Deus mihi dedit, "God has given to me."

(293; original 828): Monasticism, Engl. trans., p. 67, and S. François, p. viii.

(294; original 829): The version of Matthew Arnold, Essays in Criticism, 1st series. A recent translation is given in Robinson; the Writings of St. Francis, pp. 150 sqq., by the Franciscan, Stephen Donovan. Böhmer, p. 65, gives the Latin text.

(295; original 830): Speculum, p. 76. Domine, said Francis, minores ideo vocati sunt fratres mei ut majores fieri non proesumant.

(296; original 831): See Sabatier, S. François, pp. 80 sqq. Also Madonnet, Les Origines de l’ordo de Poenitentia, pp. 4, 21 sq. etc., who presents this feature of Francis’ society in its early days in a clear light.

(297; original 832): See Sabatier, Vie de S. François, pp. 273 sqq.

(298; original 833): This Rule has only recently been found and published in the Seraphicae legislationis textus originales, Quaracchi, 1897. See Robinson, pp. 76sqq.

(299; original 834): See Speculum, p. 181 and note.

(300; original 835): Finally by Urban IV., 1263. See Potthast, II. 1515. Affiliated houses were erected at Burgos, Spain, 1219; Rheims, France, 1220; Prague, 1235, etc.

(301; original 836): Frates et sorores de poenitentia.

(302; original 837): See Potthast, II. 1856.

(303; original 838): Potthast, I. 703. Nicolas IV., however, speaks of a rule given by Francis.

(304; original 839): See the art. Humiliaten in Herzog, VIII. 447-449, by Zöckler who quotes H. Tiraboschi, Memorie degli Humiliati, 3 vols. Modena, 1766. Sabatier, Regula antique, p. 15, upon the basis of Jacques de Vitry and other authorities, says the Humiliati were at the height of their zeal and activity in 1220. He confesses that the Tertiary Rule, the Regula Antiqua, is probably in part a copy of the Rule of the Humiliati sanctioned by Innocent III. and says, "Perhaps we have heretofore ascribed an undue originality to the Franciscan movement."

(305; original 840): See Walter Goetz, Die Regel des Tertiarierordens, in Brieger’s Zeitschrift, 1902, pp. 97 sqq.

(306; original 841): VI. 3, arma mortalia contra quempiam non recipiant vel secum ferant. This most interesting statement was changed by Nicolas IV. in 1289 so that it read, "The brethren shall not carry arms of attack except for the defence of the Roman Church, the Christian faith, or their country, or unless they have authority from their superiors." The Humiliati received papal exemption from Honorius III. against going to war. See Sabatier, Regula antiq., p. 22, Note.

(307; original 842): The development of the Tertiary order is a matter of dispute. Sabatier has recently made known two rules of the Tertiary order; the first, found in Florence, the second which he himself discovered in the convent of Capistrano in the Abruzzi. To compare them with the Rule contained in Nicolas IV.’s bull, supra montem, 1289, the Rule of Nicolas has 20 chapters, the Florentine 19, that of Capistrano 13. See the table given by Walter Goetz, p. 100. Sabatier in his edition of the Capistrano Rule, Regula Antiqua, p. 12, puts it very close to the death of Francis, between 1228 and 1234. Les Règles, etc., p. 153, goes further and puts it back to 1221, thus making it the second Rule of St. Francis. At any rate, it must for the present be regarded as the oldest form of the Rule. Goetz, p. 105, while dating the Regula Antiqua much earlier than 1289, is inclined to regard it as a compilation. In 1517 Leo X. perfected the regulations concerning Tertiary orders and divided the members into two classes, those taking no vows and living in the ordinary walks of life and those who live in convents. The best general treatment of the subject is furnished by Karl Müller, Die Anf1nge des Minoritenordens., pp. 115-171, and Madonnet who gives a convenient list of the papal utterances on the Tertiaries, Les Règles, etc., pp. 146 sq.

(308; original 843): The Observants looked to Portiuncula as the centre of the order, the Conventuals to the cathedral of Assisi.

(309; original 844): Ubertino da Casale’s interpretation of Francis’ purpose is given by Knoth, pp. 99 sq.

(310; original 845): Sabatier, Speculum, pp. li sq.

(311; original 846): Potthast, II. 1746.

(312; original 847): Ubertino, during seven days of rigid seclusion on the Verna, wrote the ascetic workArbor vitae crucifixae. See Knoth, 9-14.

(313; original 848): 2 Lempp, Anthony of Padua, p. 439.

(314; original 849): The Franciscans became guardians of the holy places in Palestine. In answer to my question put to a Franciscan in Nazareth, whether the Church of the Annunciation there was the veritable place where Mary had received the message of the angel, he replied, "Most certainly! We Franciscans have been in this land 600 years and have thoroughly investigated all these matters."

(315; original 850): See Hauck, IV. pp. 378 sq.

(316; original 851): All that we know about his life is gotten from his account of the Franciscans in England. He died about 1260. Eccleston gives the names of the nine first missionaries. Mon. Franc., pp. 5 sqq. Agnellus of Pisa stood at their head. Three of the clerics were Englishmen.

(317; original 852): Creighton, p. 107.

(318; original 853): See the descriptions of Jessopp, Coming of the Friars, pp. 21 sqq., and Brewer’s Mon. Franc., pp. xv. sq.

(319; original 8): 54 Mon. Franc., p. xli.

(320; original 855): He tells a comic story of William de Madeley, at Oxford, who, finding a pair of shoes, put them on and went to matins. Going to sleep be dreamt he was attacked by thieves, and thrust out his feet to show that he was a friar. But lo! the shoes were still on, and starting up he flung them out of the window. Another poor friar, Gilbert de Vyz, so he relates, was badly treated by the devil. It happened at Cornhill. The devil at his final visit exclaimed, "Sir, do you think you have escaped me?" De Vyz picked up a handful of lice and threw it at the devil, and he vanished. p. 13.

(321; original 856): John L’Estrange says that, at the time they were falling out of favor, one English will out of every three conveyed property to the Franciscans. Quoted by Howlett in his Preface to Mon. Franc., II. p. xxvii.

(322; original 857): According to Gasquet, p. 237, there were sixty-six Franciscan houses. Addis and Scannell’s Catholic Dict., p.388, gives a list of sixty-four. The first house of the Franciscan nuns, or Poor Clares, was founded outside of Aldgate, London, 1293, and was known as "the Minories," a name the locality still retains. At the time of the dissolution of the monasteries they had three houses in England.

(323; original 858): Ordo praedicatorum, fratres praedicatores, or simply praedicatores, as in the papal bulls and the constitutions of the order.

(324; original 859): His descent from the noble family of Guzman has been disputed by the Bollandists.

(325; original 860): Jordanus says, they went ad Marchias, which probably refers to the domain of Hugo of Lusignan, Count de la Marche, and not to Denmark, as often represented.

(326; original 861): The bull canonizing Dominic says, haereticos caritative ad poenitentiam et conversionem fidei hortabatur, he affectionately exhorted heretics to return to the faith.

(327; original 862): Potthast, I. 436.

(328; original 863): See Denifle, Archiv, 1885, p. 169, who says that Dominic took as the basis of his rule the rule of the Premonstrants and insists that his followers were canons regular. Denifle was a Dominican, and in his able article gives too much credit to Dominic for originality.

(329; original 864): This important office according to Echard at first gave to the incumbent the right to fix the meaning of Scripture at the Pontifical court. It has since come to have the duty of comparing all matters with the catholic doctrine before they are presented to the pope, selecting preachers for certain occasions, conferring the doctors’degree, etc. Wetzer-Welte avoids giving offence to the Dominicans by making the ambiguous statement, III. 1934, that Dominicgewissermassen der erste Mag. palatii wurde.

(330; original 865): Hauck, IV. 391-394.

(331; original 866): At the suppression of the monasteries under Henry VIII., the Dominicans had 68 houses in England (Gasquet, p. 237), or 57 according to Addis and Scannell, Dict., p. 301.

(332; original 867): Potthast, I. 810.

(333; original 868): See the Constitution of 1228, Denifle, pp. 212, 215.

(334; original 869): Magister generalis. In 1862 Pius IX. limited his tenure of office to twelve years. Since 1272 he has lived at St. Maria sopra Minerva in Rome.

(335; original 870): May 16, 1227. See Potthast, I. 684. Denifle makes much of this point, pp. 176-180.

(336; original 871): Denifle gives the best edition in Archiv for 1885, pp. 193-227.

(337; original 872): Denifle, pp. 181 sqq., states that the idea of poverty was in Dominic’s mind before Honorius sanctioned the order, and that it was thoroughly as original with him as it was with Francis. This view seems to be contradicted by the bull of Honorius, 1216, which confirms Dominic and his followers in the possession of goods. Jordanus, c. 27, states that the principle of poverty was adopted that the preachers might be freed from the care of earthly goods, ne predicationis impediretur officium sollicitudine terrenorum. Francis adopted this principle as a means of personal sanctification; Dominic, in order that he and his followers might give themselves up unreservedly to the work of saving souls.

(338; original 873): Caritatem habete, humilitatem servite, pauperitatem voluntariam possidete.

(339; original 874): Denifle, pp. 185 sqq.

(340; original 875): Nullus fiat publicus doctor, nisi per 4 annos ad minus theologiam audierit. Const., 1228, II. 30.

(341; original 876): Ordo noster specialiter ob praedicationem et animarum salutem ab initio institutus. Prol. to Constitution of 1228.

(342; original 877): Quoted by Denifle, p. 190.

(343; original 878): Const. II. 31-33.

(344; original 879): Paradiso, XII.

(345; original 880): See Potthast, II. 9386, 9388 (Gregory IX., 1284), etc. The Franciscans were made inquisitors in Italy and Southern France. See chapter on the Inquisition.

(346; original 881): Leo commended the rosary in repeated encyclicals, Aug. 30, 1884, 1891, etc., coupling plenary indulgence for sin with its use. He also ordered the title regina sanctissimi rosarii, "queen of the most holy rosary," inserted into the liturgy of Loreto. On the history of the rosary, see Lea, Hist. of Auric. Conf., III. 484 sqq., and especially the dissertation St. Dominikus und der Rosenkranz, by the Franciscan, Heribert Holzapfel. This writer declares, point blank, that the rosary was not invented nor propagated by Dominic. There is no reference to it in the original Constitution of 1228, which contains detailed prescriptions concerning prayer and the worship of the Virgin, nor in any of the eighteen biographical notices of the thirteenth century. Holzapfel makes the statement, p. 12, that the entire thirteenth and fourteenth centuries know nothing of any association whatsoever of St. Dominic with the rosary. Sixtus IV., 1478, was the first pope to commend the rosary; but Sixtus does not associate it with the name of Dominic. Such association began with Leo X. What has become of the author of this bold denial of the distinct statement of Leo XIII. in his encyclical of ten years before, September, 1883, I do not know. Holzapfel distinctly asserts his opposition to the papal deliverances on the rosary, when he says, p. 37, "High as the regard is in which the Catholic holds the authority of Peter’s successors in religious things, he must be equally on his guard against extending that authority to every possible question." Perhaps Father Holzapfel’s pamphlet points to the existence of a remainder of the hot feeling which used to exist between the Thomists and Scotists.

  8. 在英国，修道院尤其从乡绅阶层吸收成员。参见杰索普 (Jessopp)，161页。在莫里蒙 (Morimond)，奥地利边区伯爵之子奥托 (Otto) 曾与十五名年轻贵族留宿一夜。钟声与修士们的虔诚礼拜给他们留下了如此深刻的印象，以致他们恳求获准加入修道团体。路易六世 (Louis VI.) 之子亨利 (Henry) 访问克莱尔沃 (Clairvaux) 时，也因所见而深受感动，决定发愿入会。参见莫里森 (Morison)，《圣伯纳德传》(Life of St. Bernard)，195页。↩︎

  9. 蒙塔朗贝尔 (Montalembert) 强调，代祷是西方修道院所提供的首要服事。“他们多多祷告，始终为那些祷告不善或全然不祷告的人祷告。”《西方修士》(Monks of the West)，英译本，I. 42及下页。↩︎

  11. 利亚 (Lea) 在《圣殿骑士团的赦罪程式》(Absolution Formula of the Templars)，载《美国教会史学会论文集》(Papers of Am. Soc. of Ch. Hist.)，第V卷，以及黑费勒 (Hefele)，V. 381，已充分说明这一点。然而，迟至十三世纪，英国仍有未受司铎按立的修士。参见史蒂文森 (Stevenson)，《格罗斯泰斯特传》(Life of Grosseteste)，158页。在第五世纪，有些地方将修士的祝圣视为一项独立的圣礼。↩︎

  12. 亲眼见过中世纪会院的人对其所作的描述，展现出尘世幸福的动人图景，很难找到比这更有吸引力的了。例如圣蒂埃里的威廉 (William of St. Thierry) 对克莱尔沃会院的描述，见米涅 (Migne)，185, 248；以及彼得·德·鲁瓦 (Peter de Roya) 的描述，见米涅，182, 710。↩︎

  13. 这甚至被比作圣保罗的归信。参见艾肯 (Eicken)，324页。海斯特巴赫的凯撒 (Caesar of Heisterbach) 在《对话录》(Dialogus) 中专用一章论述归信，即发修道誓愿。第四次拉特兰会议的第13条教规，见曼西 (Mansi)，XXII. 1002，将修道人称为“宗教人士”，将修会称为“宗教”，并将进入会院称为“归信宗教”。同样，马丁五世 (Martin V.) 在1418年康斯坦茨会议上指控威克里夫 (Wyclif) 宣称，“一切宗教都起源于魔鬼”，这里所指的就是一切修会。米尔布特 (Mirbt)，《史料》(Quellen)，158页。↩︎

  15. 《编年史》(Chronicle)，VII. 35；他在那里用了很长的篇幅颂扬修士。关于会院及其设施的另一段悦人描述，可参见克罗伊兰 (Croyland) 修道院长英古尔夫 (Ingulph) 对其修道院1091年火灾的记述。他也没有忘记提及，“甚至地窖里装满啤酒的酒桶都毁掉了”。参见梅特兰 (Maitland)，286–292页。↩︎

Praeterea quosdam nocturnis horis, aliis quiescentibus sancta orationum furta quaerentes et eadem causa claustrum et ecclesiam peragrantes, multis aliquando terroribus appetebant ita ut in eorum aliquos visibiliter, irruerent et ad terram verberando prosternerent。意即：“此外，有些人在夜间趁别人安歇时，寻求暗中祷告的神圣机会，为此走遍回廊与教堂；他们有时受到种种恐怖的袭扰，甚至有形体显现出来，扑向他们当中的一些人，将他们殴打倒地。”《论神迹》(De miraculis)，I. 17；米涅 (Migne)，189, 883。↩︎

  34. “会院”(convent) 一词的本义是一个人的团体。中世纪英国法律文书中通常的措辞是“某地的院长及修道团体”。参见杰索普 (Jessopp)，119页；他提醒人们注意，中世纪英国各会院陷入了无休无止的争吵与诉讼。关于修士在修道院中的生活，参见汤顿 (Taunton)，I. 65–96。↩︎

  35. 克吕尼 (Cluny) 一度供养17,000名穷人。1117年饥荒期间，科隆附近的海斯特巴赫 (Heisterbach) 会院每日供养1,500人。在一次粮食短缺时期，伯纳德供养2,000名农民，直到收获时节。↩︎

  37. 豪克 (Hauck)，IV. 401及后，说德国像希尔德加德 (Hildegard) 和舍瑙的伊丽莎白 (Elizabeth of Schönau) 这样的女修道院长并不多。关于修士与修女腐败的控诉，来自萨克森 (Saxony)、施瓦本 (Swabia)、洛林 (Lorraine)、莱茵地区及瑞士。参见豪克所引的材料。↩︎

  38. 《主教事迹》(Gesta pontificum)，罗尔斯丛书 (Rolls Series)，70页，转引自汤顿 (Taunton)，I. 22。威廉 (William) 说：“坎特伯雷 (Canterbury) 的修士与当时英国所有的修士一样，以狩猎、放鹰和赛马为乐。他们喜爱骰子的响声、饮酒和华服，身边仆从成群，与其说像修士，不如说更像俗人。”↩︎

  39. Religio peperit divitias, divitiae, religionem destruxerunt（虔敬产生财富，财富却毁灭虔敬），《讲道集》(Hom.)，III. 96。杰索普 (Jessopp) 在《托钵修士的到来》(Coming of the Friars) 中说，十三世纪英国的修士优于他们所处的时代，这并不难相信。↩︎

  43. 《书信》(Ep.)，97；米涅 (Migne)，207，304及下页。他论到基督所容许的多种表达形式时说，ipsa varietas est uniformitatis causa（多样性本身正是统一性的根源）；这番话足以媲美现代福音联盟 (Evangelical Alliance) 倡导者的言论。↩︎

  44. 参见泽贝格 (Seeberg) 在《邓斯·司各脱》(Duns Scotus)，478及下页所引的那段非同寻常的文字。↩︎

  45. 马修·帕里斯 (Matthew Paris) 与其他英国编年史家一样，接连记载了许多这样的事例。杰索普 (Jessopp) 在《托钵修士的到来》(Coming of the Friars) 中说，中世纪英国修道制度的历史，是由无休无止的诉讼故事构成的。各会院总是与自己的主教发生纠纷。↩︎

  46. 斯塔布斯主教 (Bishop Stubbs) 在《宪政史》(Const. Hist.)，III. 329中论及英国修道院，说它们是教皇势力的堡垒，教皇支持它们，以抗衡教区主教的势力。因此，教皇从不任命英国的修道院长，也极少干预修士的选举，即使曾有过这种干预。↩︎

  47. 杰索普博士 (Dr. Jessopp)，155页，论英国修士说：“归根结底，必须承认，对十三世纪的修士而言，最大的乐趣莫过于吃喝。一座大修道院中的正餐，显然是一天中极为重要的事件。凡对这一时代的文献稍有深入了解的人，必定会注意到，十三世纪修道生活的弱点在于贪食。”不过，他说，关于醉酒的记载却很少。会院自酿的麦酒，是全年饮食中的重要项目。达勒姆主教马里斯科的理查德 (Richard of Marisco) 将诺森伯兰 (Northumberland) 埃格灵厄姆 (Eglingham) 的什一税收入赠给圣奥尔本斯修道院 (Abbey of St. Albans)，以帮助修士酿出更好的麦酒，“因为怜悯会院的酒过于淡薄”。↩︎

  48. 参见豪克 (Hauck)，III. 493。他说：“Das Mönchthum, war in Lothringen die führende Macht”（修道制度在洛林是居于主导地位的力量）。↩︎

  49. 第四次拉特兰会议 (Fourth Lateran) 指示他们每三年集会一次。↩︎

  50. 豪克 (Hauck)，III. 442。↩︎

  51. 伯里圣埃德蒙兹 (Bury St. Edmunds)、坎特伯雷的圣奥古斯丁修道院 (St. Augustine)、克罗伊兰 (Croyland)、彼得伯勒 (Peterborough)、伊夫舍姆 (Evesham)、格拉斯顿伯里 (Glastonbury) 和格洛斯特 (Gloucester) 的院长也同样如此；但格拉斯顿伯里院长享有优先地位，直到阿德里安四世 (Adrian IV) 将这一地位授予圣奥尔本斯 (St. Albans) 院长。↩︎

  52. M. 帕里斯 (M. Paris) 和其他英国编年史家，不断谴责这些托钵修会征税人的勒索行为。早在1234年，他们就在英国为教皇筹款。↩︎

  53. 赫特 (Hurter)，《英诺森三世》(Innocent III.)，IV. 238。加斯凯 (Gasquet) 在251–318页列出了一份详尽的英国修道院名录，又在211页及后叙述了在英国设有会院的各修会，并附有颇具启发性的版画。按照加斯凯的名录，单是英国就有一千五百余所会院。↩︎

  55. 豪克 (Hauck)，III. 596，认为格列高利 (Gregory) 无疑属于克吕尼修会 (Cluniac)。↩︎

  57. 豪克 (Hauck)，III. 863，列出了采用克吕尼 (Cluny) 会规或其修订形式的德国会院名录。↩︎

  58. 威廉 (William) 在希尔绍 (Hirschau) 兴建新屋，以容纳大批新加入的修士，并设立了一间抄写室 (scriptorum) 和一座图书馆。他的著作中有一部论音乐的作品《论音乐与调式》(de musica et tonis)。1556年，克里斯托夫公爵 (Duke Christoph) 将希尔绍改为一所新教学校。其建筑遭路易十四 (Louis XIV) 的军队摧毁。这些遗址是符腾堡 (Württemberg) 最古老可敬的历史遗迹之一。↩︎

  59. 贡德拉达 (Gundrada) 曾访问克吕尼 (Cluny)。她的墓碑上刻着铭文：Intulit ecclesiis Anglorum balsama morum，“她把良好品行的香膏带给了英国的众教会。”参见斯蒂芬斯 (Stephens)，254页。↩︎

  60. 亨利八世 (Henry VIII) 取缔修道院时，英国共有三十二所克吕尼修会会院。加斯凯 (Gasquet)，218页。汤顿 (Taunton)，I. 27，则提到该会有三十八所会院，并在伦敦设有三所济贫院。↩︎

  61. 即宽袖、长及脚面的外袍。主教特有的冠帽——主教冠 (mitre)，也常被赐予修道院长。最早的事例之一，是亚历山大二世 (Alexander II) 将它赠予坎特伯雷圣奥古斯丁修道院 (St. Augustine of Canterbury) 的院长。1137年，富尔达 (Fulda) 院长从英诺森二世 (Innocent II) 领受了主教冠及戒指。↩︎

  63. 《著名神迹二书》(Liber duo illustrium miraculorum)。在彼得 (Peter) 的赞助下，完成了一部《古兰经》译本。比布利安德 (Bibliander) 的修订版于1543年在巴塞尔 (Basel) 出版。这些著作收在米涅 (Migne)，第189卷，507–903；该卷也刊载了彼得的书信、讲道以及被归于他名下的赞美诗。↩︎

  64. 《致威廉的辩护书》(Apologia ad Guillelmum)。米涅 (Migne)，182，895–918。↩︎

  65. 对于这一指责，彼得 (Peter) 回答说，这类财产掌握在修士手中，远胜于落在粗野的俗人手中。↩︎

  66. 《书信》(Ep.)，I. 28；米涅 (Migne)，189，156。彼得 (Peter) 致伯纳德 (Bernard) 的若干书信保存了下来，全都强调应当践行弟兄之爱。他对犹太人的严厉抨击，却与他一贯的温厚形成了奇异的对照。参见§ 77，论向犹太人传教。↩︎

  67. 选举院长的权利从修士手中被夺走了。在阿维尼翁之囚 (Avignon captivity) 期间，教皇声称有权任命这一职务；后来法国国王也提出同样的权利要求。吉斯家族 (Guises) 掌握这所修道院的庇护权近一百年。1627年，黎塞留 (Richelieu) 被任命为院长。↩︎

  68. 克吕尼公馆 (Hotel de Cluny) 曾是显贵们下榻的地方。英国亨利八世 (Henry VIII) 的妹妹玛丽 (Mary) 在寡居期间曾住在那里；1537年，苏格兰的詹姆斯五世 (James V) 也在那里与弗朗索瓦一世 (Francis I) 的女儿玛德莱娜 (Madeleine) 成婚。克吕尼市政府购下了修道院建筑，并拆毁了其中一部分。↩︎

  69. 参见沙弗 (Schaff)，《诗歌中的基督》(Christ in Song)，以及朱利安 (Julian)，《赞美诗学》(Hymnology)。↩︎

Paris)，卢阿德版 (Luard’s ed.)，II. 399。亨利三世 (Henry III) 曾禁止修士出售羊毛。1257年，亨利二世 (Henry II) 又对羊毛课以重税，诸如此类。M. 帕里斯，IV. 324，V. 610。参见斯塔布斯 (Stubbs)，《宪政史》(Const. Hist.)，I. 541，II. 181，200。↩︎

  80. 1134年总会的一项规章，规定抄写室中必须保持安静：scriptorium. In omnibus scriptoriis ubicunque ex consuetudine monachi scribunt silentium teneatur sicut in claustro（凡修士按惯例从事抄写的各处抄写室，都应如修院内一样保持肃静）。梅特兰 (Maitland)，450页。↩︎

  81. 据说，熙笃会 (Cistercians) 完成了第一部瑞典文《圣经》译本。赫特 (Hurter)，IV. 180。↩︎

  82. 圣伯纳德 (St. Bernard) 宣称，修士的职责不在讲道，而在苦修；对他而言，城镇应当如同监狱，独处则如同乐园：quod monachus non habet docentis sed plangentis officium, quippe cui oppidum carcer esse debet et solitudo paradisus.（修士的职责不是教导，而是哀恸；城镇理当是他的监狱，独处理当是他的乐园。）他说，修士若走入世间，便是将事物颠倒过来，使独处成为监狱，使城镇成为乐园。《书信》(Ep.)，365；米涅 (Migne)，182，570。↩︎

  88. 早在1223年，总会就把这样的熙笃会修士称为“逃亡者”(fugitives)。马修·帕里斯 (Matthew Paris) 在1255年条下（an. 1255，卢阿德版 (Luard’s ed.)，V. 529），将熙笃会与多明我会 (Dominicans) 相对照，论前者说：“他们不在城市和乡镇之间游荡，而是安静地留在住所的围墙以内，服从自己的长上。”↩︎

  89. 《书信》(Ep.)，126；米涅 (Migne)，182，271。↩︎

  90. 《第一传记》(Vita prima)，III. 1；米涅 (Migne)，185，303。传记作者高弗里德 (Gaufrid) 详细描述了他的品格。他说，伯纳德 (Bernard) 是 magnanimus in fide, longanimis in spe, profusus in charitate, summur in humilitate, praecipuus in pietate（在信心上宽宏，在盼望中坚忍，在爱心上慷慨，在谦卑上卓绝，在虔敬上超群）。阿拉努斯 (Alanus) 在《第二传记》(Vita secunda)，XVII. 47，米涅，185，497中，给予他如此崇高的赞誉：humanissimus in affectione, magis tamen forte in fide（在情感上极富人情，而在信心上或许更为卓越）。↩︎

  91. 这是菲利普·沙弗 (Philip Schaff) 在《文学与诗歌》(Literature and Poetry)，282页中的评断。伯纳德 (Bernard) 在书信中屡次使用这样的表达：Nonne ego puer parvulus，“我岂不像一个小孩子吗？”《书信》(Ep.)，365；米涅 (Migne)，182，570。↩︎

  92. 该文献见米涅 (Migne)，185，622及下页。↩︎

  93. 加尔文 (Calvin) 在《基督教要义》(Inst.)，IV. 2，11中说：“伯纳德在其《论省察》(de consideratione) 中说话，仿佛真理本身在发言。”路德 (Luther) 经施陶皮茨 (Staupitz) 指引而认识伯纳德，研读了他的著作，并且时常援引他的话。克斯特林 (Köstlin)，《路德传》(Life of Luther)，I. 81。他称赞伯纳德不依靠自己的修道誓愿，而依靠基督白白的恩典得救。德尼弗勒 (Denifle) 在《路德与路德宗》(Luther und Lutherthum)，I. 56–64中，试图证明，路德将伯纳德描绘成仿佛撇下修道身份、不以之为功德，乃是歪曲事实。路德在一段激昂的文字中宣称，伯纳德临终时曾呼喊：tempus meum perdidi quia perdite vixi，“我虚度了光阴，因为我生活败坏；但有一件事使我得安慰：忧伤痛悔的心，你必不轻看。”路德说，你看，伯纳德怎样把他的修士兜帽挂在钩上，回到基督那里。据德尼弗勒所说，这两句话似乎并非伯纳德在同一时间说的。“我虚度了一生”这句感叹，出现在一篇《雅歌》讲道中，米涅 (Migne)，183，867；另一部分则是伯纳德重病时所说的。德尼弗勒指控路德为了证明自己的论点，任意摆弄伯纳德的言论；这里不是讨论这一指控的地方。只需指出，路德意在强调伯纳德唯独倚靠恩典得救，而伯纳德著作中有足够多的表述，可以支持这一立场。↩︎

  94. 同时代人极力赞扬她的虔诚。第戎 (Dijon) 圣贝尼纽斯 (St. Benignus) 修道院的院长恳求将她的遗体交给该院。圣蒂埃里的威廉 (William of St. Thierry) 论到她说：“她以敬畏上帝的心治理家务，殷勤行怜悯之事，并教养儿子们凡事顺服”，enutriens filios in omni disciplina。《第一传记》(Vita prima)，I. 1。↩︎

  96. Virtus vehementius in infirmitate ejus refulgens（力量在他的软弱中愈发显明），等等。《第一传记》(Vita prima)，VIII. 41；米涅 (Migne)，185，251。↩︎

  97. 致英国人亨利·默多克 (Henry Murdoch)，《书信》(Ep.)，106；米涅 (Migne)，182，242。Aliquid amplius invenies in silvis quam in libris. Ligna et lapides docebunt te, quod a magistris audire non possis. An non putas posse te sugere mel de petra oleumque de saxo durissimo? 等等。“你在树林中所发现的，会比在书本中更多。树木和石头会教导你那些你从教师那里听不到的事。难道你不认为自己能从磐石中吸蜜，从极坚硬的岩石中取油吗？”这些话使我们想起莎士比亚 (Shakespeare) 那几句常被引用的诗：

    在奔流的小溪中读到书卷，

    在石头中听见讲道，在万物中发现美善。↩︎

  98. 《第一传记》(Vita prima)，III. 2；米涅 (Migne)，185，306。中世纪描写自然美景的文字十分罕见。阿西西的方济各 (Francis d’Assisi) 的《太阳颂》(Canticle of the Sun) 是一个例外。弗赖辛的奥托 (Otto of Freising) 陪同腓特烈·巴巴罗萨 (Frederick Barbarossa) 前往罗马接受帝冠，满腔热忱地讲述德意志人的军容，却对罗马的壮丽景象或古迹只字未提。见费希尔 (Fisher)，《中世纪帝国》(Med. Empire)，II. 229。↩︎

  107. Si despicit frater meus carnem meam, ne despiciat servus Dei animam meam. Veniat, proecipiat, quicquid praecperit, facere parata sum. “即使我的兄弟鄙弃我的肉身，也求上帝的仆人不要鄙弃我的灵魂。让他来，让他吩咐；无论他吩咐什么，我都准备遵行。”《第二传记》(Vita secunda)，VII. 22；米涅 (Migne)，185，482。可曾有过比这更温柔恳切的姊妹哀求？↩︎

  149. 在巴黎附近的康佩尔 (Campell)，司铎不少于五十人，英诺森三世 (Innocent III.) 将其人数减为二十二人。见胡尔特 (Hurter)，III. 375。canonicus saecularis（在俗咏祷司铎）和 regularis（律修咏祷司铎）这些用语，在十二世纪以前并未出现。在此之前，他们被称为 clerici religiosi, clerici regulares, clerici professi, clerici communiter viventes（修道教士、守规教士、发愿教士、共同生活的教士）等等。德尼弗勒 (Denifle) 在1886年的《文学与教会史档案》(Archiv für Lit. und Kirchengeschichte)，174页中如此说。他引述阿莫特 (Amort)，《律修与在俗咏祷司铎的古代规制》(Vetus disciplina canonicorum regul et saecul.)，威尼斯 (Venice)，1747年，I. 333。↩︎

  150. 克罗德加纳 (Chrodegana) 为其座堂参议会的教士设立了公共食堂和公共寝室。1059年、1063年的罗马会议建议司铎将收入归公。↩︎

  151. 相传英诺森二世 (Innocent II.) 于1139年规定所有律修咏祷司铎都须遵守这一会规。埃利奥 (Helyot)，II. 21。↩︎

  152. 英诺森 (Innocent) 在1243年12月16日的一道诏书中提到 regula S. Augustini et ordo（圣奥古斯丁会规及修会）。见波特哈斯特 (Potthast)，954页。法国最著名的律修咏祷司铎修道院，是圣维克多 (St. Victor) 修道院。↩︎

  153. 布里斯托尔 (Bristol) 的主教座堂由原来的圣奥古斯丁 (St. Augustine) 修道院改建而成。奥古斯丁派咏祷司铎 (Augustinian, or Austin, canons) 在英国也称黑衣咏祷司铎 (Black Canons)。他们在那里极受欢迎。他们在英国的第一所会院——科尔切斯特 (Colchester) 的圣博托尔夫 (St. Botolph’s) 会院——约建于1100年。修道院遭取缔时，他们在英国有一百七十所会院，在爱尔兰则更多得多。加斯凯 (Gasquet)，225页。见W. G. D. 弗莱彻 (W. G. D. Fletcher)，《牛津的黑衣修士》(The Blackfriars in Oxford)。↩︎

  154. 见胡尔特 (Hurter)，III. 238。↩︎

  155. 修道院解散时，他们在英国有三十二所会院。加斯凯 (Gasquet)，241页。↩︎

  156. 吉尔伯特会 (Gilbertines) 约于1140年由桑德灵厄姆 (Sandringham) 的本堂牧师圣吉尔伯特 (St. Gilbert) 创立，仅限于英国。修道院遭取缔时，该会有二十六所会院。男女修院共用一座教堂。↩︎

Ger.)，XII.，670页及后；赫特尔 (Hertel) 的德译本载于《德意志古代史家丛书》(Geschichtschreiber der deutschen Vorzeit)，莱比锡 (Leipzig)，1881年。另见豪克 (Hauck)，IV. 350–66；J. 冯·瓦尔特 (J. von Walter)，《法国最早的巡回传道者》(Die ersten Wanderprediger Frankreichs)，第II卷，莱比锡，1906年，119–129页；韦策尔—韦尔特 (Wetzer-Welte) 中的“普雷蒙特雷会”(Praemonstratenser) 条，X. 267及后，以及“诺伯特”条，IX. 448及后；赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 中的“普雷蒙特雷会”(Praemonstratenser) 条，XV. 606及后，及该处所列文献；还有加斯凯 (Gasquet)，《英国普雷蒙特雷会》(The Engl. Duesterdieck) 博士，《研究与评论》(Studien u. Kritiken)，1903年，506页。↩︎

  165. 该修会的徽记是一个上方立着狮子的地球，题有格言 Stat crux dum volvitur orbis，“世界转动，十字架屹立”。↩︎

  166. 以下传说是为解释布鲁诺 (Bruno) 的决定而编造的。1082年，他参加了巴黎圣母院咏祷司铎雷蒙德 (Raymond) 的丧礼。当诵读到“Quantas habes iniquitates et peccata?”（“你有多少罪孽和过犯？”）时，死者起身回答：“justo dei judicio accusatus sum”（“我已在上帝公义的审判下被控告。”）次日，重复诵读这些话时，死者再次起身，高声说：“justo dei judicio judicatus sum”（“我已在上帝公义的审判下受审判。”）第三天，死者第三次起身，呼喊说：“justo dei judicio condemnatus sum”（“我已在上帝公义的审判下被定罪。”）这件事曾被收入《罗马日课经》，但1631年奉乌尔班八世 (Urban VIII.) 之命删除。赫根勒特 (Hergenröther) 说，加尔都西会士仍为这一传说辩护。《教会史》(Kirchengesch.)，II. 353。↩︎

  167. 可敬者彼得 (Peter the Venerable) 谈到一次访问沙特勒兹 (Chartreuse) 的经历时说，《书信》(Ep.)，VI. 24：inaccessibiles pene nivibus et glacie altissimas rupes non abhorrui，“面对那些因冰雪而几乎无法攀登的高峻岩壁，我并未退缩。”胡尔特 (Hurter) 的描述，IV. 150，使这个地方显得颇为诱人。↩︎

  170. Vestes vilissimas ac super omne religionis propositum abjectissimas ipsoque visu horrendas assumpserunt. “他们穿上最粗劣的衣服，其卑陋超过一切修道生活所要求的程度，单是看上去就令人惊骇。”可敬者彼得 (Pet. Ven.)，《论神迹》(De miraculis)，II. 28。↩︎

  171. 加尔都西会士中曾有人想转入纪律较宽松的其他修会，这一动向受到英诺森三世 (Innocent III.) 的严厉斥责。胡尔特 (Hurter)，IV. 161。↩︎

  172. Medicinis, excepto cauterio et sanguinis minutione perraro utimur（除烧灼和放血外，我们极少使用医疗手段），胡尔特 (Hurter)，IV. 154，引自吉戈 (Guigo) 的《会章》。为医疗目的而放血，似乎在修道院中颇为普遍。海斯特巴赫 (Heisterbach) 修道院也实行此法，见海斯特巴赫的凯撒 (Caesar of Heisterbach)，《对话录》(Dial.)，XI. 2。据《蒂龙的伯纳德传》所载，一些修道院有这样的习俗：修士若患头痛或其他身体疾病，便请院长按手在他们身上，信赖他行神迹的能力使他们得医治。见瓦尔特 (Walter)，《法国最早的巡回传道者》(Die ersten Wanderprediger Frankreichs)，莱比锡 (Leipzig)，1906年，II.，50页。↩︎

  173. 然而，他们至少出过四位枢机、七十位大主教和主教，也拥有以艺术珍品著称的富丽教堂，如那不勒斯 (Naples) 的教堂，以及大量使用青金石的帕维亚 (Pavia) 教堂。见胡尔特 (Hurter)，IV. 158。↩︎

  175. “就我们的习性和气候而言，这种纪律过于严苛，这种孤寂过于可怕。”杰索普 (Jessopp)，《托钵修士的到来》(The Coming of the Friars)，125页。↩︎

  176. 法国大革命期间，该修会在法国遭到取缔。不过，1816年修士获准返回大沙特勒斯 (Grand Chartreuse) 修道院，每年向政府缴纳3000法郎租金。1903年的《结社法》再次解散了这所母院。当时院内有一百五十名修士。其中一些去了皮埃蒙特 (Piedmont)，另一些去了西班牙的塔拉戈纳 (Tarragona)，并在那里设立酒坊，酿造他们珍贵的利口酒。↩︎

  177. Ordo B. M. V. de Monte Carmelo（加尔默罗山荣福童贞女马利亚修会）是英诺森四世 (Innocent IV.) 所赐的名称。何诺留三世 (Honorius III.) 在1226年批准该修会时，称其弟兄为 fratres eremiti de monte Carmelo（加尔默罗山隐修弟兄）。韦策尔—韦尔特 (Wetzer-Welte)，II. 1966–1976的“加尔默罗会士”(Carmelite) 条，以及赫尔措格 (Herzog)，X. 84–88的“加尔默罗会士”(Karmeliter) 条，均有良好的介绍，并附文献目录。波特哈斯特 (Potthast)，I. 第7524号。↩︎

  178. 从地中海驶近海岸时，或从拿撒勒周围的山丘望去，加尔默罗山 (Mt. Carmel) 上的修道院都十分醒目。现有建筑建于1828年，距海法 (Haifa) 有一小时步程。拿破仑 (Napoleon) 在叙利亚战役期间曾将先前的院舍用作医院。↩︎

  179. 《加尔默罗会之镜，或以利亚修会史》(Speculum Carmelitarum seu historia Eliani ordinis)，4卷。安特卫普 (Antwerp)，1680年。↩︎

  180. 1725年，本笃十三世 (Benedict XIII.) 准许该修会在圣彼得大教堂为以利亚立像，等于对其主张给予了某种认可。像上刻着：Universus ordo Carmelitarum fundatori suo St. Eliae prophetae erexit.（全体加尔默罗会士为其创始人圣先知以利亚敬立。）↩︎

  181. 加尔默罗会士常被称为圣衣兄弟会 (Brotherhood of the Scapulary)。圣衣是一种覆盖胸背的无袖短衣，最初是修士劳作时套在其他衣服外面穿的。这种衣服屡次成为教皇谕令讨论的对象，直到1892年利奥十三世 (Leo XIII.) 时仍是如此。自1587年以来，7月16日被定为特别的圣衣节，也是圣母的节日之一。布罗卡尔 (Brocard) 曾就圣衣的正确使用撰写一书：《加尔默罗山圣母圣衣敬礼训导集》(Recueil des instructions sur la devotion au St. Scapulaire de Notre Dame de Monte Carmelo)，根特 (Gand)，第4版，1875年。西门·斯托克 (Simon Stock) 去世时享年一百岁。↩︎

  182. 赫尔根勒特—基尔施 (Hergenröther-Kirsch)，《教会史》(Kirchengesch.)，II. 362，说此事是作为一种“虔诚的见解”——fromme Meinung——而提出的。↩︎

  183. 这道诏书的原件尚未发现，其真实性在罗马天主教会内部引起了激烈争论。马利亚的有关话语是：Ego mater gratiose descendam sabbato post eorum mortem et, quot inveniam in purgatorio, liberabo. “我这位母亲将在他们死后的安息日施恩降临，凡我在炼狱中找到的，我都要释放。”怀疑这道诏书真实性的一个理由，是马利亚在其中应许赦罪。保罗五世 (Paul V.) 于1613年下令，应当宣讲这种“虔诚的信仰”。见韦策尔—韦尔特 (Wetzer-Welte)，X. 1444–1447，“安息日特恩”(Sabbatina) 条。↩︎

  184. 根据克莱孟八世 (Clement VIII.) 1593年的决定，赤足修士成为独立修会，自行选举总会长。胡尔特 (Hurter)，IV. 213，在其关于加尔默罗会的简述末尾说，他们惯于夸大到可笑地步的事物中，包括其院舍和修士的人数；他们声称有7500所院舍、180,000名修士。↩︎

  186. 安东尼会士 (Antonites) 视圣安东尼 (St. Anthony) 为厩养牲畜的主保；这一观念在意大利民间颇为流行。《菲利普·沙弗传》(Life of Philip Schaff)，56页及下页，举有这种信仰的一个实例。↩︎

  187. 三一会士 (Trinitarians) 又称马图林会士 (Maturines)，名称源自他们在巴黎圣马图林礼拜堂 (St. Mathurine’s chapel) 附近的会院。他们在英国有少数会院。彼得·诺拉斯科 (Peter Nolasco) 和彭纳福特的雷蒙德 (Raymond of Pennaforte) 创建了一个宗旨相同的西班牙修会，即 Ordo B. V. M. de Mercede redemptionis captivorum（赎俘仁慈圣母会）。见胡尔特 (Hurter)，IV. 219。↩︎

  188. 最后一位女院长于1799年去世。自1804年以来，丰特夫罗 (Font Evraud) 修道院被用作关押囚犯的场所。英国的亨利二世 (Henry II.) 和狮心王理查 (Richard Coeur de Lion) 均葬于丰特夫罗。关于该修会的文献，见赫尔措格 (Herzog)，VI. 125，以及J. 冯·瓦尔特 (J. von Walter)，《法国最早的巡回传道者：修道制度史研究，阿布里塞尔的罗伯特》(Die ersten Wanderprediger Frankreichs, Studien zur Gesch. des Mönchthums, Robert von Abrissel)，I.，莱比锡 (Leipzig)，1903年。↩︎

  199. non visiones in somnis, nec dormiens, nec in phrenesi, nec corporeis oculis aut auribus exterioris, nec in abditis locis percepi, sed eas vigilans, circumspiciens in pura mente oculis et auribus interioris hominis, 等（我领受这些异象，并非在梦中、睡眠中或狂乱中，也非凭肉体的眼睛或外在的耳朵，亦非在隐秘之处；而是在清醒、环顾四周之时，以纯净的心灵，凭里面之人的眼睛和耳朵领受）。《认识道路》(Scivias)，I.，序言 (Praefatio)，米涅 (Migne)，197，384。↩︎

  210. 1190年，狮心王理查 (Richard Coeur de Lion) 前往巴勒斯坦途中驻留西西里 (Sicily)，因约阿希姆 (Joachim) 的声望而派人召他前来。这位院长向他解释了约翰关于敌基督的预言，宣称敌基督已经降生，日后将登上使徒宝座，并敌挡一切称为上帝的。德·霍维登 (De Hoveden)，英译本，II.，177页及后。↩︎

  211. 约阿希姆 (Joachim) 曾在一篇小论著中陈述他反对伦巴德 (the Lombard) 的观点，会议曾提及此文。见曼西 (Mansi)，xxii.，以及赫费勒—克内普弗勒 (Hefele-Knöpfler)，V. 880及下页。↩︎

  212. 约阿希姆 (Joachim) 在1200年开列的一份清单中，列出了这三部著作，也提到反对犹太人以及论基督教信条的著作。肖特 (Schott)，170页，统计归于他名下的真作与伪作共二十四部。被判为伪作的，包括自十三世纪中叶起方济各会士广泛使用的《耶利米书注释》和《以赛亚书注释》。这些书称罗马为巴比伦，对教皇表现出强烈敌意；这样的表述与约阿希姆的真作相冲突。它们还充斥着对后来确曾发生的事件的详尽预言。德林格 (Döllinger)，369页，说：“假如这些书是真作，那么众多预言如此准确地应验，便构成预言史上最奇妙的现象。”↩︎
